Teaching Data Science to Secondary Students: The Mobilize Introduction to Data Science Curriculum. In J. Engel (Ed.), Promoting Understanding of Statistics About Society. |
| Playful Learning in the Community: An interactive tour of community based early learning installations | 2026 | ISLS 2026 | Andres S. Bustamante, Maria J. Anderson-Coto, Karlena D. Ochoa, Annelise Pesch, Julie Salazar | None found | Playful Learning Landscapes (PLL) transforms everyday community spaces like parks, bus-stops, and grocery stores into conduits for caregiver-child interactions and early learning. During this workshop, attendees will visit a series of PLL installations in Santa Ana, CA, which were co-designed by researchers from UC Irvine and Temple University, and families from the Santa Ana Early Learning Initiative, a local grassroots non-profit community organization. Attendees will engage with the PLL installations and learn about the design process and impact of the installations on community members and caregiver child interactions at PLL sites. The session will also demonstrate how this project was conducted in partnership with the City of Santa Ana’s Parks and Recreation and Public Works departments who own and maintain the installations and have begun to replicate them using public funds in other community spaces. Challenges, lessons learned, plans for sustainability, and practical and theoretical insights will be discussed. Playful learning in the community: An interactive tour of community based early learning installations Informal learning environments like children’s museums, zoos, and maker spaces are powerful contexts for supporting young children’s science, technology, engineering, and math (STEM) learning and identity. Unfortunately, children from low-income families have less access to these spaces due to a variety of barriers like admission prices, transportation challenges, or even an awareness of their existence. In addition to these barriers, linguistically and ethnically minoritized families can feel alienated in these spaces which rarely center their languages, cultural values, and practices. Playful Learning Landscapes is an international initiative that brings informal learning installations to everyday spaces like parks, bus-stops, grocery stores, laundry mats, medical clinic waiting rooms, sidewalks, and walls (Hassinger-Das, 2018; Bustamante et al., 2019). Playful Learning Landscapes promote the caregiver-child conversations and interactions that decades of developmental and learning sciences research suggest lead to learning, such as conversational turns, STEM language, and hands-on engagement (Bustamante et al., 2020; Hassinger-Das et al., 2021; Pesch et al., 2025). In addition to designing PLLs based on the latest research in developmental and learning sciences, they are also designed in partnership with local families, so the installations build from families’ values, routines, and cultural practices (Bermudez et al., 2023). Understanding learning as a cultural process situates family’s participation in learning environments and design as dictated by values, routines, and cultural practices (Bang & Medin, 2010; Rogoff et al, 2007). Leveraging cultural assets, practices, and values can therefore increase relevance and significance for families, in turn increasing inclusivity, sustainability, and engagement. Partners The PLL work in Santa Ana, CA, is conducted in the context of a long-term research practice partnership between the Santa Ana Early Learning Initiative (SAELI), the University of California Irvine, and the City of Santa Ana. SAELI is a grassroots non-profit community organization bringing together local families, non-profit organizations, and leadership from the Santa Ana Unified School district to support families with children between 0-9 years of age. SAELI is led by director Wendy Gomez and uses a multigenerational approach through which promotoras (e.g., Chen et al., 2023) promote wellness through initiatives on child development, connecting families with resources, and local advocacy projects. On the university side, these projects are led by UC Irvine S chool of Education Professors Andres Bustamante and June Ahn and Temple University Psychology Professor Kathy Hirsh-Pasek. This research team brings expertise in child development, early education, participatory design, and research practice partnerships. The City of Santa Ana supported the project through their Parks and Recreation Department and Public Works Department, with support from the City Manager’s Office, Mayor's Office, and City Council. Other organizations and businesses included in this work were Northgate Gonzalez Market grocery chain, the Santa Ana Unified School District, First Five Orange County, and Orange County Health Care Agency. In this RPP, we endeavored to center families’ voices in all phases of the process from designing the installations, to deciding their locations, to the evaluation research, and dissemination of the findings. Importantly, trust and relationship-building with families were central to the design sessions. Personal stories and family values were shared through informal conversations over meals. These informal opportunities to connect allowed trust to be built over time, which uniquely positioned researchers and families as equals. We believe this allowed families to share openly and addressed traditional power dynamics between researchers and families. Decisions were taken jointly through parallel conversations with partners and interested parties, requiring transparency, trust-building, multiple channels of communication, rapid problem-solving, adaptation, and other forms of invisible relational labor. However, tensions emerged throughout the process particularly when navigating the intersection of large bureaucracies including federal funding, university systems, and city government. The project faced multiple challenges, including changing project timelines and material constraints, changes in organizational leadership, physical limitations of the public spaces designated for installations, and the complex social and political contexts of the community. Given existing power differentials between governmental partners and community stakeholders, our university team played a critical intermediary role, advocating on behalf of the community in certain cases. Whereas in other cases, our community partners stepped up and advocated for the project using their influence as voting constituents and activists in their community. Ultimately, the political capital of all stake- holders was necessary to bring this project to fruition. Research design in the community The Santa Ana PLLs were supported by an Advancing Informal STEM Learning (AISL) award from the National Science Foundation. The project began in 2020, and due to the pandemic, all design sessions in the first year of the project were held on Zoom. We held seven 90-minute co-design sessions where we learned about families histories and experiences with their community spaces, and used participatory design activities such as bags-of- stuff, photo-elicitation, story boarding, and storytelling to encourage families to share their goals and routines in their community spaces (for a detailed description of design process see Bermudez et al., 2023; Garcia et al., 2022; Chew et al., 2022). Through an inductive thematic analysis of these co-design sessions, we surfaced community values, practices, and aspirations such as promoting intergenerational learning, and increasing family time. As part of these themes, we also surfaced ways in which families' own routines and practices already involved STEM concepts and practices. We iteratively combined these themes and existing practices with the science practices and crosscutting concepts from the Next Generation Science Standards (NGSS) and principles from developmental science for creating high-quality early learning environments that are active, engaging, iterative, social, meaningful, and joyful (Hirsh-Pasek et al., 2015). While a single design could not meet all the criteria, we sought balance across community values, developmental principles, and science learning goals (see Bermudez et al., 2023 for more detail). Further, we were intentional to align these practices with SAELI’s three step design model that included engaging parents in discovery & dreaming, designing, and creating a sustainability plan. Using these design goals and guiding frameworks, we conducted prototype ideation sessions with SAELI families and then subsequent sessions where we created low-fidelity mock-ups of the prototypes for play testing and iteration during the second and third years of the project. Children and parents were interviewed to hear their feedback on the designs and learn about their experiences playing with the physical prototypes. These sessions were key to evaluate factors from usability to how and if families’ interactions aligned with their values and practices. These sessions were held in person at their local Community Center, started with a shared meal, followed by a design activity, reflections and share outs. Through these sessions we iterated and refined the prototypes until they were ready to be shared with our fabrication partners. Fabrication and launch For fabrication, we contracted a Los Angeles based museum installation design shop. We worked closely with their engineers to adapt the co-designed prototypes for durability, usability, safety, cost efficiency, and regulation compliance. All designs were reviewed by City of Santa Ana Parks and Recreation or Public Works departments and UC Irvine’s Facilities Management department. We worked collaboratively with the City of Santa Ana and S AELI to decide where the installations would go, taking advantage of existing city renovation efforts to save costs and create efficiencies when possible. We also worked with legal representation from UCI and the City of Santa Ana to execute a Memorandum of Understanding that the PLL installations were being donated to the city by UCI and the City of Santa Ana would own and maintain them. This process took approximately two years from the beginning of the fabrication process to installation in public spaces. The installations were implemented between Fall 2024 and Spring 2025 with a grand opening ribbon cutting ceremony held on May 10th, 2025, as part of the NSF’s 75th anniversary celebration and Mother’s Day in many Latin American countries. The ceremony was hosted at Madison Park in Santa Ana and attended by the Mayor, Director of Parks and Recreation, several city council members, and the Dean of the UCI School of Education. Data collection Our data collection protocol included baseline observations of STEM language and caregiver child interactions at each of the five installation sites pre-installation as well as a matched comparison site (e.g., for a bus-stop installation we observed a comparison bus stop in the same neighborhood). We also trained SAELI parents to collect surveys at all PLL and control sites of caregiver’s self-efficacy in teaching STEM to their children, perceptions of safety and learning opportunities in their community spaces, and attitudes around the connection between play and learning. We then performed the observational and survey data collection protocol post- installation to examine the impacts of the PLL’s on caregiver child interactions and experiences in community spaces. While post-installation data collection is on-going we expect to present findings from these analyses at the ISLS workshop in June 2026. For a more detailed description of our analytical plans please refer to our project pre-registration on the Open Science Framework. Workshop details The tour will be hosted by UCI Associate Professor and project PI Andres Bustamante, UCI Professor and project Co-PI June Ahn, SAELI Director Wendy Gomez, and several SAELI parents who participated in the design and data collection processes. The hosts will offer insights into the project’s co-design approach, the partnership with the city (challenges and opportunities), parents’ roles as designers, data collectors, and project advocates, impact results, and future plans for policy agenda towards sustainability and scaling. The timetable of activities and sites to be visited are shown below (Table 1). We will also discuss several cross-site design considerations, including Playful Learning Principles, culturally responsive design, the use of public spaces as learning environments, multigenerational engagement, attention to accessibility, and low-burden participation that aligns with families’ everyday routines. Table 1 Timetable of Activities Hour Activity 8:00 am Meet at the conference hotel at 8am PST on June 15th, 2026. We will depart on a charter bus to tour the five PLL sites in Santa Ana. 8:30 am Arrive at Santa Ana, touring the 5 different sites (see text below). 11:30 am End of the tour. The charter bus will be taking people back to the conference hotel. Site 1: Madison Park. Madison Park features the largest of the Santa Ana PLL installation, Parkopolis, the life-sized board game for math and science learning. Parkopolis is a giant board game where children spin life sized dice and advance around the board completing math and science games like playing patterns on musical pipes, and measuring how far you can jump on a giant ruler. Through the co-design process, we learned about games like Basta, a category game that many SAELI families play, and integrated them into Parkopolis games. During the co-design sessions, many families expressed concerns about visiting their neighborhood parks and cited the bathroom facility in the back of Madison Park as a hotspot for illicit activities like drug use and prostitution. In response, the city demolished those bathrooms replacing them with new bathroom facilities in another part of the park, and we constructed Parkopolis where the old bathrooms were transforming a hot spot for criminal activity into a hub for early learning, a powerful example of community voice leading to impact in public infrastructure. Site 2: Abacus bus stop. T he giant abacus at the bus stop on the corner of Main Street and McFadden Avenue in Santa Ana invites families to engage in conversations around numeracy and arithmetic. Many families in our design session reference using an abacus to learn math when they were children in Mexico and other countries in Latin America and wondered why their children’s schools didn’t use an abacus. This installation is a powerful symbol of scalability because it was not funded by our NSF grant. The city of Santa Ana was renovating bus stops in this area and due to the awareness of our projects, leveraged existing funds to execute the design. Site 3: Northgate Gonzalez market. Northgate is a family owned local grocery chain that many of the SAELI families shop at. During our co-design sessions, we used families stories about grocery shopping with their parents growing up or with their children now to develop signage that promote math and science interactions. Examples include a conversion chart between pounds and kilos at the deli and using your senses to pick out the best fruits and vegetables in the produce section. This installation is unique because it demonstrates how local business and the private sector can engage and support this work. Site 4: Angels Park. Angels Park features two PLL installations, La Lotería which is a giant version of the bingo style game that is very popular with local families, and How Tall Am I? which is a statue where children can measure their height in comparison to California animals. Both of these installations are a great example of intuitive design and leveraging existing knowledge to create engaging STEM learning experiences. How Tall Am I? invites immediate engagement through its visual design and integrated measurement markers, requiring only minimal instruction in the form of a single guiding question. Similarly, Lotería is among the most popular family games in this community, lengthy instructions are not necessary. Instead, we simply embed short STEM activities into the playing cards for families to integrate into the game play. In addition to supporting informal STEM learning, both of these installations were co-designed with the intention of supporting intergenerational learning which is a core value of Latine families. Site 5: Washington elementary school. The last stop on the tour will visit the Yo Veo (I See) Mural, an interactive art exhibit where children look for hidden objects that reflect local cultural and community activities. This is another example of a local institution adopting a project design. This mural was funded by the Santa Ana Unified School District, who were renovating this school and directed funds for murals. This school also features a different project, Fraction Ball, a redesigned basketball court for math learning. Future directions During the May 10th ribbon cutting event, the City of Santa Ana made an official proclamation that May will be Early Childhood Education month in the city in recognition of this innovative work. Building on this momentum, we are working with city officials on a resolution to integrate PLLs into the city’s on-going process for new public amenities leveraging existing budget from local institutions. We plan to position SAELI as drivers of future PLL efforts where they engage local community members in a design process of new public amenities that center early learning and build from local families’ funds of knowledge. This work represents a scalable and community centered vision for designing public infrastructure to promote child development and civic engagement. Invitation to ICLS participants This session examines the evolution of a project and partnership from initial creation through implementation, using this project as a live case study to surface challenges, lessons learned, and future directions from different points of view. In doing so, we demonstrate how partnering with purpose can generate meaningful community impact and offer useful insights for other scholars and initiatives seeking to engage in collaborative, community- centered work. | Bodies, Participation, and Power: Learning as a Site of Inclusion, Exclusion, and Resistance | 2026 | ISLS 2026 | Morgan Vickery | Vickery, M., & Peabody, E. (Under Review). Addressing the dinosaur in the room: An unexpected evolution from partnership to participation. | Youth bring diverse ways of knowing, communicating, and creating meaning to school, yet classrooms often privilege a narrow slice of these practices—treating difference as deficit and leaving many disabled, neurodivergent, queer, or trauma-impacted youth at the margins of participation. These exclusions affect belonging and access to civic, community, and disciplinary life. My research investigates how to design asset-oriented, participatory, and body- inclusive learning environments for young adolescents, where questions of identity and belonging are especially acute. Drawing on sociocultural and critical learning theories, participatory design-based research, and social models of ability and the body, I examine how multimodal repertoires can be recognized and leveraged as learning resources. This agenda advances three strands focused on designing multimodal learning environments that prioritize learner and educator dignity: (1) expanding conceptions of adolescent participation and literacy, (2) designing inclusive multimodal technologies, and (3) positioning youth as co-designers and co-theorists of their learning. Strand 1: Expanding conceptions of youth participation This strand is grounded in my experiences and those of current adolescents regarding secondary schools’ narrow conceptions of participation. Secondary schooling has privileged print and verbal literacies while devaluing embodied, affective, and multimodal avenues for meaning-making. These framings disproportionately exclude multiply-marginalized adolescents and reinforce deficit orientations and hegemonic norms. In my previous work, I employ a social model of disability to explore how schools ‘disable’ and ‘enable’ certain bodies, calling for researchers to shift responsibility for student performance away from the individual and onto the design of the system in which they operate (Vickery, 2023). In my dissertation, I introduce a five- dimensional inter-theoretical framework that conceptualizes the body as simultaneously thinking, feeling, observed, politicized, and autonomous. I also provide methodological guidance for conducting humanizing, dignity-affirming research with youth living in non-normative bodies (Vickery, 2025a) and employ critical resistance narrative analysis to examine how adolescents navigate and resist normative embodied expectations in schools (Vickery, 2025b). Moving forward, I expand normative notions of participation in youth civic education. I extend this work into civic and technical domains—motivated by the current sociopolitical climate. Such work demands multidisciplinary and multimodal competencies that cannot be siloed within individual subject areas. To these ends, I hope to support adolescents’ learning of media, data, digital, and scientific literacies through engagement with culturally relevant issues. Building from epistemic practices in science and engineering education (e.g., evidence evaluation, systems reasoning, modeling), I see opportunities to extend these into literacy/language education (rhetorical device usage, media & data literacy, interpretation of misinformation), social studies/history (complex sociopolitical systems, causal mechanisms), and computing education (engineering ethics, data privacy, transparency, ethical AI adoption). As political systems increasingly disenfranchise multiply-marginalized youth, these literacies become not abstract skills but conceptual and tangible tools for civic and social change. A society of care must acknowledge and design against these barriers. Strand 2: Designing inclusive multimodal technologies This strand is rooted in my experience as a UX designer and researcher, where access and inclusion were often addressed retroactively rather than prioritized from the start. In educational technologies, this reinforces deficit- oriented assumptions about learners whose practices do not align with normative expectations. This work centers on learners’ histories, needs, and desires in the edtech design process, ensuring technologies are proactive, responsive, and adaptive to learners’ needs. In my recent work (Vickery & Danish, 2025), I share my rapid design and iterative refinement of a mixed-reality embodied learning environment for a reverse-inclusion summer camp for youth with moderate-to- severe disabilities, aimed at identifying and addressing barriers to expression and participation. In my 2024 work o n the GEM-STEP project (Vickery et al., 2024; NSF Grants #1908632 & #1908791), I led my team in integrating trauma-informed pedagogies into the facilitation and evaluation of mixed-reality embodied activities (where students use motion-tracking technologies to collaboratively model complex scientific phenomena), demonstrating how centering comfort, choice, and agency can amplify students’ capacities for participation. In Zhou et al. (2025), we investigate how elementary learners' group-divergent embodied practices afford opportunities to learn about complex scientific phenomena. As a Visiting Research Scientist funded by the Engage AI Institute, I design and develop narrative-based, AI-scaffolded, gamified experiences for middle school students that are context-relevant and engage learners in critical thinking about food and environmental justice (Humburg et al., 2025). I build on my dual background in software engineering and UX design to critically engage with how ‘cutting-edge’ technologies such as AI, XR, and asynchronous learning tools are implemented at scale. My prior work has often relied on retrofitting inclusion into technologies – moving forward, I hope to (1) establish trusting partnerships with schools and communities, (2) conduct on-the-ground needs assessments, (3) co-design contextually grounded solutions with these needs prioritized at the onset, and (4) iteratively evaluate implementation. Access and inclusion are not endpoints but baselines; innovation must be defined by—and accountable to—those most impacted. Strand 3: Positioning youth as co-designers and co-theorists of their learning This strand addresses a tension across education and research alike – that too often, youth are positioned as passive users of and participants in designs they had no role in shaping. Thus, these designs (which here include pedagogies, activities, tools, communities, and theories) fail to provide the necessary scaffolding for youth to thrive and learn within them. My work takes an asset-oriented stance, treating adolescents’ perspectives and repertoires of practice as valuable resources for designing more sensitive, equitable, and emancipatory experiences for youth who would otherwise be forced to (at best) change themselves for the sake of compliance. In prior work, I’ve offered autoethnographic accounts of how my traditional notions of research-practice partnerships were challenged and remade in a collaboration with local paraprofessionals, special educators, and youth with disabilities (Vickery & Peabody, Under Review). In my dissertation Article 2 (Vickery, 2025c), I drew on Vygotskian notions of mediation and affect to understand what affective, sensory, and lived experiences are activated for youth with non-normative embodied experiences in the presence of various instructional design elements (i.e. various activity/task framings, technologies, proximity of embodiment, sensory demands, forms of movement, & participation structures). Further, Article 3 positions youth as co-theorists of a framework for dignity-affirming, body-inclusive embodied learning environments (Vickery, 2025d). This strand responds to a recurring theme across my projects—that “grown-ups just don’t get it.” This perceived ignorance strains opportunities for authentic educator–youth relationships and deepens youth distrust of educational systems. To address this, I aim to develop organized, protected “designer spaces”—digital and physical—for youth to co-design community-facing projects and professional development for educators and youth-service providers. Theoretically, this strand validates youth knowledge as legitimate civic and pedagogical insight; methodologically, it redefines youth’s role in research beyond that of participants to that of educators and researchers with the means to help adults “do better.” References Humburg, M., Danish, J., Kim, Y., Hmelo-Silver, C., Dragnić-Cindrić, D., Ryan, Z., Glazewski, K., & Lester, J. | Towards Supporting Technical and Non-Technical Skills Development by Using Multimodal Debriefing System After Multi-User VR-Based Simulation Training | 2023 | CSCL 2023 | Vitaliy Popov, Yaxuan Li, James Cooke, Alanson Sample, Michael Cole | Bracq, M. S., Michinov, E., & Jannin , P. (2019). Virtual reality simulation in nontechnical skills training for healthcare professionals: a systematic review. Simulation in Healthcare, 14(3), 188-194. | This NSF-funded study aims to develop and evaluate a novel debriefing system that aims to capture and visualize multimodal data streams from multi -user VR environment that evaluate learners’ cognitive (clinical decision -making) and behavioral (situational awareness, communication) processes to provide data -informed feedback focused on improving team - based care of patients who suffer sudden medical emergencies. Through this new multimodal debriefing system, instructors will be able to provide personalized feedback to clinicians during post-simulation debriefing sessions. Overview and motivation Caring for critically ill patients is a difficult and complex task that requires healthcare professionals to make cognitively demanding decisions under information - and time -constrained circumstances. To be able to act precisely and quickly, they need freq uent, high-fidelity training opportunities that offer meaningful feedback on the skills that are essential for quality team -based clinical care. Simulation-based training (as depicted in Figure 1 panel ‘A’) has been a successful pedagogical strategy to edu cate clinicians and prepare them to manage low - frequency, high-risk events in a safe setting. Traditionally, simulation-based training has involved using a mannikin as a patient in a simulated patient care setting. To improve accessibility, resource utilization, and the learner experience, there is a growing movement towards using virtual reality ( VR)-based simulation training in healthcare. With these goals in mind, the study team has developed the multi -user Virtual Reality for Cardiac Arrest Resuscitation (VR -CR) platform (NSF IIS- 2202451). This platform leverages state -of-the-art voice recognition and motion capture technologies to allow four participants to be in the same ‘virtual room’ and work together to care for a virtual cardiac arrest patient - all under realistic time pressure and rapid workload changes (Figure 1, panel ‘B’). However, the ongoing challenge for both manikin- and VR-simulation training is the constant and real-time human observation required to provide learners the assessment and feedback needed to drive learning and skill development. This makes this simulation not optimal ly efficient or scalable (Bracq, Michinov, & Jannin, 2019; Martinez -Maldonado et al., 2020). Importantly, this limitation results in learners receiving feedback that is of variable quality - often generalized, inconsistent, and highly dependent on simulation instructors (Figure 1, panel ‘C’). Figure 1 Panel A shows a typical simulation-based training environment where participants practice on a manikin and realistic equipment. Panel B shows a screenshot of the multiple person virtual reality training system (VR -CR) that this project will build upon. Panel C depicts a typical debriefing scenario where instructors provide immediate feedback to participants on based on their performance. Panel D shows our vision for the multimodal debriefing system. Study objectives To enhance the current high-stakes learning environment, behavioral data from the video-recorded observation of trainees and event stream log data generated by the VR system will be collated with fine -grained sensor data on visual attention, emotional arousal, and verbal participation to offer new visualizations of the team and individual performance. The following research activities have been developed to fulfill the study objectives: 1) Conduct a human -centered design study with trainees and instructors to refine the concept of the debriefing system and identify needs and preferences for trainees and instructors.  To inform design and implementation of the multimodal debriefing system, the study team will observe and record a cardiac arrest training scenario using the VR -CR simulation and post -simulation debriefings. Guided by the theoretical underpinnings and the feedback model (Figure 2), we will perform think-aloud, interviews and co-design sessions with trainees and instructors to (a) extract meaningful features from video, event stream log data generated by the VR system, and sensor data on visual attention, emotional arousal, and verbal participation, and (b) conduct exploratory pattern identification using these extracted features and limit the amount of less vital data. 2) Iteratively engineer and evolve an unobtrusive multi -modal data and sensor collection system . The meaningful features inferred from the previous step will be leveraged in data processing using machine learning algorithms. The goal is to develop a set of reliable analytical metrics assessing team and individual performance. We will design, implement, and evaluate a series of small-scale usability trials of debriefing system prototype. 3) Conduct a quasi-experimental study with a pretest-posttest non-equivalent control group to evaluate the potential of the multimodal debriefing system to improve clinical knowledge, skills, and teamwork performance. This step will help determine the utility, learning gains, and feasibility associated with adding dynamic behavioral tracking to the conventional assessment of clinical skills during VR-based simulation training. Figure 2 Theoretical underpinnings and the feedback model adapted from Gabelica & Popov, 2020  as a necessary precursor to explain why and how feedback modifies behavior in post-simulation debriefings. Learning, teaching and technology innovations For learning, understanding markers that may predispose trainees to errors or delays in therapeutic interventions will provide significant insight into a more holistic assessment of individual and team learning processes and provide unique opportunities for feedback, practice, and/or remediation. For teaching, it remains an open question how instructors can best leverage these rich multi -modal data streams to help trainees reflect on their own performance. The main technological innovation will be the development of multimodal methods of sensing and detecting speech, gaze behavior, cognitive and affective states, and using algorithmic approaches to detect user activity and model behavior in VR simulations. This is the first study using multi -user VR-based simulation to combine multiple data modalities especially for the noncognitive competencies into one innovative comprehensive debriefing system. References Bracq, M. S., Michinov, E., & Jannin , P. (2019). Virtual reality simulation in nontechnical skills training for healthcare professionals: a systematic review. Simulation in Healthcare, 14(3), 188-194. Gabelica, C., & Popov, V. (2020). “One size does not fit all”: Revisiting team feedback theories from a cultural dimensions perspective. Group & Organization Management, 45(2), 252-309. Martinez-Maldonado, R., Echeverria, V., Fernandez Nieto, G., & Buckingham Shum, S. (2020). From data to chi conference on human factors in computing systems (pp. 1-15). | Making Space for Critical Action: Re-visioning Computational | 2024 | CSCL 2024 | Thinking | None found | While school makerspaces promise to inspire and excite, the challenge of meaningfully integrating them into schools remains. Guided by a philosophy of praxis that stresses the need for education to interweave theory, action, and reflection to advance posi tive social change in our communities (Freire, 1970), this paper reports on the co-design of a school space called the Critical Action Learning Lab (CALL) for inclusive making to support computational thinking and critical action through curriculum -informed learning. |
| How Students Ground Mathematics Through Distributed Gestures: Designing Computer-Supported | 2025 | CSCL 2025 | Chaeyeon Kim, Fangli Xia, Joel P. Beier, Michael I. Swart, Kelsey E. Schenck, Mitchell J. Nathan | None found | We explored how students interact with a computer-supported collaborative learning (CSCL) system for embodied learning in geometry. With The Hidden Village-Online (THV-O), fifteen high school students (six dyads, one triad) used movements to construct directed actions that depict geometry transformations collaboratively. THV-O is a web-based CSCL video game that uses a motion-capture to detect players’ movements, save movements they create, and play those created by their peers. By observing dyads’ co-design processes and semi-structured reflections, researchers found two important shortcomings in the design of THV-O for embodied learning: (1) players experienced difficulty in conveying geometric transformations through poses, which could hinder their ability to represent generalizable properties of space and shape; and (2) players faced challenges in jointly co-constructing a single mathematical object, limiting their ability to collaborate actively and develop shared mathematical ideas. We offer guidelines for an improved design of embodied collaborative reasoning and learning. |
| Technology-Mediated Lesson Study to Improve Teacher Outcomes | 2025 | CSCL 2025 | Michelle Hudson, Max Longhurst, Joshua Stowers, Clara Smith, Austin Moore | Cheung, W. M., & Wang, W. Y. (2014). Does lesson study work? A systematic review on the effects of lesson study and learning study on teachers and students. International Journal for Lesson and Learning Studies, 3(2), 137-149. \| Kennedy, M. M. (2016). How does professional development improve teaching? Review of Educational Research, 86(4), 945-980. https://doi.org/10.3102/0034654315626800. \| Smith, C. M., Leary, H. M., Jensen, J. L., & Sansom, R. L. (2024). Ten years of three-dimensional science and its implementation in the secondary classroom: A scoping review. Journal of Science Teacher Education, 1-19. https://doi.org/10.1080/1046560X.2024.2411467. | Professional development is crucial for teachers to build their skills and adopt three- dimensional science. Effective professional development should be ongoing, collaborative, and spread over time to ensure lasting changes in teaching practices. Rural teachers often face isolation and less training compared to their urban counterparts, limiting their opportunities for collaboration. This research explored how technology-mediated lesson study (TMLS) helped rural teachers improve their teaching abilities and three-dimensional science skills. TMLS adapts lesson study using technology, allowing teachers to collaborate remotely. Based on four years of evidence, this research developed and refined the steps of TMLS with rural science teachers. This work also aimed to improve their three-dimensional science teaching abilities by forming small, collaborative groups that co-design, implement, and evaluate lesson plans together. Overall, TMLS offers rural teachers a valuable platform to enhance their teaching skills, collaborate meaningfully, and overcome the challenges of isolation. |
| Design and Play in Real Time: A Participatory Design Approach for Autistic Children to Design Online Play with Peers | 2025 | CSCL 2025 | Yiqi Xiao, Jiabei Xu | Tonge, B., Wong, V. V., Zwaigenbaum, L., & Bölte, S. (2016). Functioning and disability in autism spectrum disorder: A worldwide survey of experts. Autism research : official journal of the International Society for Autism Research, 9(9), 959–969. https://doi.org/10.1002/aur.1592 \| Khatab, S., Hijab, M. H. F., Othman, A., & Al-Thani, D. (2024). (2020). Self-regulated learning and learning analytics in online learning environments: A review of empirical research. In Proceedings of the Tenth International Conference on Learning Analytics & Knowledge (pp. 524-533). Acknowledgments This research was supported by the National Science Foundation AI Institute Grant No. DRL-2112635. Any opinions, findings and conclusions or recommendations expressed in this material are those of the authors and do not necessarily reflect the views of the National Science Foundation. | This paper presents a participatory design approach for children, designers, and educators to collaboratively create inclusive digital play environments that foster interactions between neurodiverse and neurotypical children. Integrating generative AI (GenAI) empowers children to actively contribute to game design while engaging in the play with their peers without adult mediation, highlighting the potential of such methods to foster inclusive collaboration among children, between children and adults, and between children and technology. A Call for creating inclusive social opportunities online Like other children, autistic children often desire peer friendships (Rodda & Estes, 2018) yet frequently face social challenges (Lord & Magill-Evans, 1995). These include difficulties with language, sensory processing, and interpreting social cues—such as jokes, eye contact, or facial expressions (Ghanouni et al., 2019; Aherne, 2023)— which can lead to reduced or atypical peer interactions. Given these challenges in face-to-face communication, online spaces offer alternative avenues for social expression. Research shows a strong preference among autistic children for screen-based media, especially video games (Mazurek & Engelhardt, 2013). As Barrington Campbell, an autistic adult, shared: “If you are autistic, this can feel more than stressful... Online gaming means autistic people can play in an environment we feel comfortable in and can control” (National Autistic Society, 2024). However, autistic children also face heightened online safety risks compared to peers (Macmillan et al., 2020). Mainstream digital spaces rarely support neurodiverse communication and often neglect their well-being in interactions with non-autistic users. Few games, whether commercial or academic, are designed to connect autistic and neurotypical children through shared interests in gameplay (Spiel & Gerling, 2021; Xiao, 2024). The research team that designed Zopy, a collaborative iPad game that successfully fostered friendship among autistic students without adult mediation, encouraged designers to create more games that reflect the diverse spectrum of social behaviors in peer collaboration (Boyd et al., 2015). Building on this, more design approaches are needed to guide the development of such inclusive applications. This paper proposes a participatory approach to digital play, involving collaboration among children, educators, designers, and GenAI. The rationale and methods for each type of collaboration are illustrated through the design case Together Tales, a collaborative storybook making game (Xiao, 2024). Collaboration among children, educators, and designers Why involve children? Traditionally, adults have controlled all aspects of the design process, even when the intended users are children. As Druin (2002) noted, children have distinct preferences, curiosities, and needs that differ from those of adults. Their input is therefore essential for designing effective products. Involving children not only improves outcomes but also ensures they feel valued and heard. Despite growing recognition of children as design partners, research involving autistic children remains limited. Most games for this group adopt top-down approaches with educational or medical goals, often overlooking their interests (Spiel & Gerling, 2021). Given the social challenges autistic children face, it is vital to include them in designing social games tailored to their needs. It is also worth noting that autistic children, like other neurodiverse groups, may be natural design partners. They often exhibit traits commonly labeled as “gifted,” such as excelling in creativity, visual-spatial skills, and attention to detail (Armstrong, 2010), making them highly capable contributors to design processes. How to involve children? In many participatory design (PD) practices, researchers acknowledge that children often struggle with verbal communication, particularly when dealing with abstract concepts (Druin, 2002). This issue is likely to be even more pronounced in collaborations between autistic children and adults. As a result, drawing is frequently used as an alternative form of contribution to the design process (Hunt et al., 2023). In the approach proposed here, children express ideas through drawings and then select AI-generated elements that match their vision. These are directly incorporated into the final design. The technical implementation of this GenAI collaboration is discussed in later sections. In Together Tales, children are prompted by animations within the game to draw characters they imagine appearing in specific story scenarios. They then select the generated images that best match their vision. The selected images retain certain features and are integrated into the subsequent storylines, influencing the progression of the narrative. Why involve educators? It is a common practice to involve educators in the participatory design process involving children (Piedade et al., 2024; Ey & Spears, 2020), to leverage their expertise and insights, which help ensure pedagogical guidance. In the context of this paper, educators primarily function as stakeholders, providing critical insights that bridge pedagogical goals with the needs and interests of the children. How to involve educators? While understanding why educators should be involved is relatively straightforward, addressing how to involve them effectively requires deeper consideration. Excessive involvement by educators may reduce the freedom and space for children to contribute, while insufficient involvement risks a lack of critical information in the design. How can we achieve an appropriate level of participation? The participatory design process proposed in this paper borrows from the concept of guided play (Weisberg et al., 2016). It highlights the strategy that adults create a setting that emphasizes a specific learning goal while still allowing children the freedom to explore and interact within the environment independently. Research shows that compared to a free setting and a highly structured setting, children’s active participation in discovery, combined with appropriate scaffolding from a knowledgeable adult, a higher level of engagement and better learning result (Sim & Xu, 2015). In Together Tales (Fig. 1), educators define the overarching storyline—the goal of the journey for two players, structure of activities in which children engage during digital play. These activities may include collaborative drawing, assembling puzzle pieces, organizing photos to reconstruct a story, or other creative tasks. While the framework provides guidance, the specifics—such as what to draw, the themes of the photos, or the approach to completing the activity—are shaped by the children’s prior choices and left open for their exploration. This balance between structure and freedom ensures meaningful engagement and allows children to take ownership of their creative process. Figure 1 A Demonstration of The Mechanism of Together Tales. The role of designers/researchers Rather than focusing solely on the final product, designers and researchers should prioritize creating innovative processes and frameworks that position users and stakeholders as active, independent contributors. They are also responsible for assessing the technical feasibility of stakeholders’ ideas to ensure the process runs smoothly. Collaboration between autistic and neurotypical children Why involve both groups? Peers support autistic children’s play in ways adults cannot (Jones, 1991), yet few digital play environments reflect neurodiverse interests. Many games focus narrowly on autistic children’s social challenges rather than fostering inclusive or neurodivergent-centered experiences (Spiel & Gerling, 2021; Xiao, 2024). Two major misconceptions contribute to this gap. The first is that only autistic children benefit from peer interaction. Jones (1991) identified self-disclosure, trust, and companionship as key to friendship satisfaction. A global study of 2,555 autism experts emphasized traits like morality, trustworthiness, and kindness in autistic individuals (de Schipper et al., 2016)—qualities that make them valuable friends. Without opportunities to engage, neurotypical children may miss out on meaningful relationships, thus they too need to learn how to interact and collaborate with autistic peers. The second misconception is that autistic children are not capable of forming strong friendships. Though their styles may differ, research increasingly shows that autistic children build meaningful relationships with both neurotypical and autistic peers (Rodda & Estes, 2018). Therefore, creating more opportunities for positive interaction is essential to support relationship-building between neurodiverse children. How to involve both groups? Collaborative play—where players coordinate actions toward a shared goal—effectively fosters social interaction between autistic and neurotypical children (Reuter et al., 2014; Khatab et al., 2024). Moreover, Piedade et al. (2024) found that creating such artifacts enhances self-determination in autistic children. While few studies compare artifact co-design to digital collaboration within the screen in terms of social outcomes, the approach of “collaborative making through game” shows strong potential and merits further exploration. In Together Tales, children collaborate on playful design decisions, such as choosing what kind of backpack to include, determining the type of animal characters they encounter, and deciding the sequence of photos displayed on a wall. These decisions collectively weave a unique narrative. At the end of the game, children receive a co-authored, printable digital storybook as a tangible artifact of their collaboration. Collaboration between children and GenAI Why involve GenAI? The development of AI technology has significantly transformed and advanced educational practices. For example, AI can assist teachers in designing and delivering personalized, timely, iterative, and enriching learning experiences (Nguyen, 2023). However, a significant portion of current methodologies view AI as an extension of roles traditionally held by educators, behavior analysts, or other adult experts, aiming to achieve previously unattainable efficiencies (Amiri et al., 2017; Shahamiri & Thabtah, 2020). Few approaches aim to help children enhance their own abilities, limiting their opportunities to participate meaningfully in the design process and reducing their agency within support systems. Moreover, while discussions have highlighted the potential of AI as a creative assistant in participatory design (Zhao, 2024), research focusing on its application for children— especially autistic children—is scarce. Despite this, AI has been used in various contexts to support youth in creating original work outside the PD framework. Ali et al. (2021) demonstrated how GenAI supported K-12 students and educators in co-designing learning materials, boosting teacher confidence. Such cases underscore AI’s potential to enhance children’s creativity and support its further exploration in participatory design with autistic children. Figure 2 Character Creation to Collaborative Storytelling in Together Tales. How to involve GenAI? Through the versatile application of GenAI, generative image model (GIM) empowers children to transform their sketches into content-consistent images. Vision-language model (VLM) further enhances this process by converting visual outputs into descriptive text, integrating these capabilities with large language model (LLM) and carefully crafted prompts, AI can generate adaptive visual content tailored to children’s preferences, all while maintaining alignment with educator-defined scaffolds to ensure meaningful engagement. In Together Tales, generative AI is implemented using ComfyUI combined with Stable Diffusion and ChatGPT APIs (Fig. 2). Summary This paper presents an innovative design process through Together Tales—a storybook game designed to foster collaboration between autistic and non-autistic children. The process unfolds across three levels of collaboration. First, among children, collaborative making fosters neurodiverse peer-to-peer interactions, cultivating authentic and inclusive social connections. Second, between children and educators, the approach ensures that pedagogical goals are met while providing children with the freedom to create and collaborate independently, striking a thoughtful balance between guidance and autonomy. Third, between children and generative AI technologies, the integration of GenAI showcases AI’s potential to enhance their creative agency and further enables them to co- create unique experiences and products for themselves with adults and their peers. Discussion This participatory design model holds great potential for broader application—not only for autistic children but also for those with ADHD, dyslexia, and other neurodiverse profiles, in collaboration with neurotypical peers. The educational principles underpinning this design, such as collaborative play and scaffolding, are rooted in general education practices, suggesting their adaptability across various neurotypes and learning contexts. While this approach emphasizes children’s contributions to one aspect of game design, future work will explore AI integration across the full design process, building toward a more comprehensive child-centered framework. Upcoming iterations will also include structured observations and post-play interviews to assess its effectiveness. References Aherne, D. (2023). The pocket guide to neurodiversity. Frontiers in Communication, 9. https://doi.org/10.3389/fcomm.2024.1440384 Using Markov Chain Analysis to Study the Relations Between Emotions, Cognitive Actions, and Performance in Collaborative Learning Ashwin T S, Vanderbilt University, ashwindixit9@gmail.com Caitlin Snyder, University of Detroit Mercy, snydercr@udmercy.edu Srigowri Mayasandra Prasanna, Vanderbilt University, srigowri.mayasandra.prasanna@vanderbilt.edu Naveeduddin Mohammed, Vanderbilt University, naveeduddin.mohammed@vanderbilt.edu Gautam Biswas, Vanderbilt University, gautam.biswas@vanderbilt.edu Abstract: Collaborative problem-solving (CPS) in STEM+C education involves cognitive coordination and emotional regulation during joint tasks. Prior research has examined discrete affective states in learning environments but less is known about how these emotions evolve over time and affect CPS behavior. This study investigates the temporal dynamics of five emotions—engagement, confusion, boredom, delight, and frustration—using Markov Chain analysis of data from high school pairs building computational models in the C2STEM environment. Emotional transitions aligned with cognitive processes, seen in interaction patterns like PLAY, ADJUST, and BUILD, to analyze affect during modeling. Results show that emotional trajectories closely relate to cognitive actions, including construction, simulation testing, and debugging. Transitions that maintained engagement linked to productive collaboration and stronger performance, while ongoing frustration and boredom indicated disengagement progress. |
| Frame Alignment and Feedback in CT-ified Science Classrooms | 2026 | CSCL 2026 | Connor Bain, Uri Wilensky | None found | While computational thinking (CT) is an integral part of modern scientific inquiry, STEM teachers struggle to incorporate these ideas in classrooms. We report on the efforts of a design-based implementation research project in which teachers participate in a constructionist co-design process to design and then implement CT -ified STEM units in their classrooms. Using feedback teachers left on student responses from these unit implementations, we utilize a basic bag-of-words model to show that teachers did specifically attend to student learning at the intersection of CT and STEM. We then further explicate this evidence through a qualitative think-aloud interview with one of the teachers who designed and implemented a curriculum based around computational models in biology. What we find is evidence that STEM teachers in CT -ified classrooms can assess both CT and S TEM skills at the same time  though this intersection still needs additional attention in terms of tools and PD for teachers. |
| Designing AI Partner-Embedded Learning Environments to Support AI Literacies | 2026 | CSCL 2026 | Mon-Lin Monica Ko, Marcelo Worsley, Michael Alan Chang, Chelsea Chandler, Sierra Rose | Akkerman, S. F., & Bakker, A. (2011). Boundary Crossing and Boundary Objects. Review of Educational Research, 81(2), 132-169. https://doi.org/10.3102/0034654311404435 (Original work published 2011) | As AI technologies become increasingly integrated into schools, questions arise about what students should learn about AI and how AI-powered tools can support collaborative learning. This symposium reports on the Institute of Student-AI Teaming (iSAT), which designs AI partners and curricular activity systems to promote AI literacy and collaboration. Unlike most classroom AI tools designed for 1:1 interactions, iSAT develops AI partners as social collaborators that both facilitate group learning and serve as objects of inquiry —helping students “look under the hood” of AI systems. Our multi -year co-design process across three U.S. states engages teachers, students, and parents to build integrated systems of curriculum, technology, and professional learning. The symposium’s six posters span AI design, infrastructure, curriculum, and teacher learning, illustrating how AI can be productively embedded within classroom collaboration. Together, they advance understanding of how AI systems can meaningfully support and transform collaborative learning. Objective of the session As AI technologies become increasingly embedded in education (Reich & Dukes, 2024), so do questions about what students should learn about AI systems (AI literacy) and how AI -powered tools may support (or hinder) student learning. While there is growing excitement about AI’s potential to transform education, decades of research highlight repeated failures of earlier innovations to do so. Scholars argue these failures often stem from underappreciating the infrastructural nature of technology and overestimating its power to drive change in classroom practice (Roschelle et al., 2010). First, technology is infrastructural in that its value lies not in its particular content but in its ability to generate new representations of disciplinary ideas and create opportunities for students to engage more deeply in those ideas (Hegedus & Kaput, 2003). Second, like previous technologies, today’s AI tools will not transform education on their own. Their success depends on how curriculum activity systems—teacher professional learning (PL), instructional materials, and technology —work together to reshape learning environments (Vahey et al., 2013). Their success also depends on how teachers and students work together within the system to support learning. This symposium reports on work from the Institute of Student-AI Teaming (iSAT), an institute that aims to design AI -powered tools and curricular activity systems that promote collaborative knowledge building and student learning around AI -literacy specifically (D’Mello et al., 2024, OECD, 2025). Our project intentionally combines both AI -literacy and AI as a tool for collaborative learning in ways that will enable the AI -partner to serve simultaneously as a collaboration support and an object of inquiry as students think critically about how the AI works. That is, our AI partners play a dual role of supporting student collaboration and as a tool for “looking under the hood” at how AI systems work. Supporting collaborative learning is a longstanding goal of both the ISLS and CSCL community, and is crucial because we know that learning is inherently a social process. Yet, the dominant approach to incorporating AI inside K-12 classrooms focuses on 1:1 interactions between students and AI systems. iSAT’s approach is radically different: we aim to develop AI as a social, collaborative partner that works alongside students and teachers to deepen their engagement with one another. This goal has required that our team engage in multi -year co-design processes to better understand the needs and desires of multiple users 556 and stakeholders (e.g. teachers, students, parents) as we work to develop and refine AI partners, curriculum materials, and professional development to support the development of AI literacies. Over the past five years, our work has involved working with stakeholders in districts across three U.S. states to conceptualize, develop, enact, and refine our curricular activity system. This session will focus on the design of iSAT’s curricular activity system to deepen the field’s conversation about the role of AI -systems in supporting collaborative learning. Across the posters, we will describe how our co- design practices, back -end models, classroom pilot studies, professional learning, and curricular routines and designs worked toward our emerging understanding of the utility of these tools for supporting collaborative learning. Overview of the symposium The 6 posters that constitute the symposium represent our collective efforts to take seriously what it means for AI systems to partner within the curricular activity systems of secondary schools. Poster 1 will detail the trajectory of co-design for our first AI partner, the Community Builder (CoBi) and how the ongoing design, implementation and analysis of the co- design space informed the values of our institute. Poster 2 will present on the design, development, and pilot studies of our second Jigsaw Interactive Agent (JIA) in secondary classrooms and how productive uncertainty was leveraged to design the AI partner interventions to suppo rt small group jigsaws. In Poster 3, we will report on the architecture and analytic pipeline that underlies our two existing AI partners, documenting how the pipeline infrastructure, state detection and dialogue policies inform the AI- partner outputs and interventions. Posters 4-6 address how we built both curriculum materials and teachers’ capacities to leverage them so that the AI partners could be meaningfully integrated into everyday classroom activities. In Poster 4, we examine how curricular routines can support students' sensemaking of their own collaborative practices. In Poster 5, we show how our PL design supported teachers’ learning about AI, our AI literacy curriculum and pedagogical approaches that center student sensemaking. Finally, Poster 6 describes the design and refinement of our curriculum materials that focus on AI literacy, a process guided by co- design, adaptation and refinement with teachers in their classrooms. Scholarly significance This session is both timely and essential. First, AI tools are becoming increasingly embedded in schools and in our world. At the same time, few teachers are currently prepared to teach about AI, use AI -enabled tools, and adopt student-centered, collaborative pedagogies—what we call a triple innovation challenge . We also know that external forces (e.g. social and news media) shape students’ understanding of AI, and that schools/educators play a less significant role (OECD, 2025) This session highlights a new approach to supporting AI literacy through the design  of curricular activity systems where AI Partners are meaningfully embedded to support AI literacies through collaborative learning. Structure of the session The symposium will be organized as a structured poster session. The chair of the session will first provide a brief overview of the session (4 mins). Each presenter will give a one -minute elevator pitch to introduce their project (6 mins). Presenters will then move to their respective posters; participants can view posters, demos (if applicable) and engage in discussion. We will invite participants to engage in two “rounds” of poster-viewing (30  mins each), so that co-presenters have a chance to view other s’ posters . Our discussant will synthesize the posters and pose questions for the audience to discuss, focusing on the challenges and affordances of AI -powered systems in supporting collaborative learning in schools (20 minutes). CoBi Origins: Co-design narratives as boundary objects to translate Co-design findings into interdisciplinary implementation Michael Alan Chang Objectives Collaborative and participatory design is increasingly used to inform the design and implementation of AI -Ed tools that are responsive to the needs and lived experiences of interest holders (Ravi et al., 2025). An AI -Ed research team hosts a co -design context, completes an analysis of it, and then  responds to the co- design efforts through implementation. While this sequential and linear process simplifies the already complex and interdisciplinary process of building AI-Ed tools, it can constrain both the types of analysis that can be conducted within the co-design context and the ways in which stakeholders can engage in the ongoing design of AI-Ed tools 557 (Chang, under review). Here, I share our efforts in iSAT, where there were parallel, longitudinal efforts to (a) develop and analyze a three- year co-design study with high school youth to surface their hopes and concerns around the use of AI in schools and (b) respond to the findings of those co- design studies to develop AI partners and curricular activity systems that surround the use of the technology. Here, I ask: how does the ongoing design, implementation, and analysis of co- design spaces shape the des ign activities of an interdisciplinary research institute? Perspectives Here, I view stories about co-design activities as an evolving boundary object that guides how large-scale AI-Ed implementation teams take on co-design findings (Star & Griesemer, 1989). Boundary objects are conceptualized as “how artifacts can fulfill a specific function in bridging intersecting practices” (pg. 134, Akkerman & Bakker, 2011). As Star (2010) clarifies, a boundary object is characterized by interpretive flexibility across different contexts, allowing each context to tailor the object to its own needs locally.  Finally, boundary objects follow particular trajectories within organizational contexts; once created, boundary objects evolve through local tailoring, standardize over time, and, finally, fade away as the standardization practices begin to constrain possible local adaptations. In turn, new boundary objects are then created. |
