Developmental Review, 30(1), 1–35. | Focusing on developing maker -identities, especially for historically marginalized students in the computational field, can empower them to recognize and take ownership of their space in the field. Drawing from identity related literature in maker and computing related fields we identified seven factors of maker -identity - interest and motivation, competence and performance, confidence and self -efficacy, recognition, utility value and meaningfulness, perceptions of community, and external factor s. Using this, we  analyzed semi -structured interviews of students who participated in our summer makerspace camp to understand how these identity factors manifested in their reflections of the camp . We tie back our finding s of positive impacts on maker -identity structures to  the design structures of our makerspace such as co-design of the space, use-modify-create strategies, and open-ended design projects. |
| Factors That Impact the Implementation of a Game Based Curriculum and Adaptations Teachers Design to Address Them | 2023 | ICLS 2023 | LuEttaMae Lawrence, Daniel I. Garcia, Karlena Ochoa, Sabrina Valdez, Kreshnik Begolli, Andres S. Bustamante, June Ahn | Durlak, J. A., & DuPre, E. P. (2008). Implementation matters: A review of research on the influence of implementation on program outcomes and the factors affecting implementation. American Journal of Community Psychology, 41(3), 327-350. | Teachers play critical roles in both co-design processes and intervention studies, yet both approaches have distinct theoretical underpinnings that impact how teachers interact and what they contribute . In this paper, we analyze the co -design and implementation of a n intervention called Fraction Ball (FB) , a game -based curriculum that teaches fractions and decimals to elementary students. We present preliminary analys is of the contributions from teachers who participated in the co -design or intervention study. We argue p articipating in different aspects of the project will illuminate different factors that might affect the implementation, and lead to different adaptations to address these factors . Teachers identified nine factors as  influential in the implementation of FB across four levels, including school, teacher, students, and intervention levels. The findings demonstrate that teachers contribute differently based on their role in the project and has implications for how we co -design interventions and frame teachers’ roles in the future. |
| Curriculum as Seed: Designing and Supporting the Use of Open Education Resources to Promote Teacher Agency and Innovation | 2023 | ICLS 2023 | Mon-Lin Monica Ko, Arturo Cortez, William R. Penuel | Glaser, B. (2008). The constant comparative method of qualitative analysis. Grounded Theory Review, 3(7). \| Shulman, L. S. (1987). Knowledge and teaching: Foundations of the new reform. Harvard Educational Review, 57, 1–22. | As open education resources (OERs) continue to proliferate, teachers will likely utilize these freely available resources to design and enact disciplinary instruction. While OERs have transformative potential in shaping classroom instruction, their open -access and availability also introduces new questions about how teachers will learn to take up and utilize these curriculum materials. In this conceptual paper, we build on a metaphor introduced in the field of human -computer interaction to reconceptualize the relationships among OERs, teachers, students, and the local context. We expand on how this new metaphor foregrounds aspects of curriculum use that have been backgrounded in previous conceptual frameworks, including the timescale and evolution of curriculum use, teacher creativity and agency, and the role of educational systems in shaping curriculum use. Curricula as tool for education reform Designing and disseminating high-quality curricula has historically been a main vehicle for supporting large-scale reforms to transform mathematics and science learning in K-12 classrooms (Stein & Kim, 2009). While   curricula such as NextGen Storylines and OpenSciEd (OSE, 2022) are examples of such an effort within science education, there are also other disciplines, including Artificial Intelligence (AI) education, that are taking similar approaches to curriculum development. Curriculum materials can be a key lever for supporting disciplinary learning because they serve as boundary objects between reform initiatives (e .g., NRC Framework, NGSS) and the design of classroom instruction (Penuel et al., 2011). The tasks, tools, and representations that constitute the curriculum communicate what aspects of the discipline are worthy of pursuit in the classroom, as well as (pot entially) new visions of teaching and learning (Sherin et al., 2004). Teachers play a pivotal role in bringing these curriculum materials to life by using their actions, ideas and experiences in ways that transform the written curriculum into the enacted curriculum (Ball & Cohen, 1996). Open education resources (OERs) are materials that can be freely shared and, with many licenses, freely adapted. As they become more prevalent in the educational landscape, there are new opportunities and challenges for using materials as resources for supporting disciplinary instruction. For example, OERs remove financial burden as a barrier to accessing high quality materials. However, materials tend to represent a specific approach to disciplinary instruction and cannot b e designed to meet the needs and interests of all students across various education settings. In addition, many embody visions for teaching and learning that may be unfamiliar to many educators. Thus, teachers will need to engage in professional learning e xperiences that help highlight the principles underlying these materials to enact them in sustainable and responsive ways within their classrooms. Teachers, curriculum materials, students, and local contexts must be considered together for curriculum materials to become a lever for both teacher and student learning (Short & Hirsh, 2020). Our conjecture is that the proliferation of OER resources opens up new opportunities to engage with different metaphors that can help us transform the relationships among teachers, students, curriculum materials, and the contexts in which they are enacted. One prominent conceptual model in both science and mathematics education foregrounds different orientations of teachers with respect to how they engage with curriculum materials (Remillard, 2005). Remillard’s framework outlines the knowledge that teachers and curriculum materials bring and how these forms of knowledge interact to transform the designed curriculum into the planned and then enacted curriculum. In this framework, the students and contexts are seen as inputs that shape the enacted curriculum (see Figure 3, Remillard, 2005). New metaphors are needed, both in the age of OERs and given what we know about curriculum adoption and sustainable change to practice of te aching. First, teachers orient in different ways to and learn from curricula in different ways, and their use of curriculum materials is varied and dynamic (Arias et al., 2016). While curriculum designers have been increasingly and intentionally embedding educative features (Davis & Krajcik, 2005) to support teacher learning in the context of curriculum use, attending to all these features each time curriculum is used is unfeasible. Supporting teachers’ use of educative features must be supported intentionally through professional learning, and yet, we seldom acknowledge that this process takes time, as teachers utilize and become more familiar with the materials themselves. One would expect that the relationship between the teacher and curriculum would shift over time, as each additional use is an opportunity to better understand how the teacher can leverage their knowledge and experience and attend to different curricular features when enacting the curriculum materials. Second, existing frameworks do not provide guidelines or account for how teachers might become more agentive in their use of the curriculum materials over time. Current forms of professional learning around the use of OER materials largely focus on guiding teachers through a curriculum story line, although recent efforts have shifted to emphasizing local adaptations that explicitly address the lived experiences of students. Understanding how teachers make sense of the design principles that undergird curriculum materials and learn to make principled adaptations (DeBarger et al., 2013) in alignment with these principles is a timely and critical issue to take up within the learning sciences community. Finally, current frameworks do not sufficiently account for the role of contexts in shaping cur riculum use. Teachers make sense of curriculum materials through (and not in the absence of) the lens of the policies, practices, and instructional routines that are at work in their teaching contexts (Allen & Heredia, 2020). Moreover, students’ sensemaking of the curriculum is shaped by the experiences they build within their community. Decades of learning sciences research suggests that disciplinary instruction ought to leverage and build on these robust and localized experiences for disciplinary learning to be meaningful (Barab & Luehmann, 2003). In other words, local contexts are not just backdrop in which curricula are enacted; they should inform the design of the curriculum if they are to be used in responsive and locally relevant ways (Buxton, 2010). For these reasons, the field needs new metaphors to help us rethink the ways in which teachers, students, curriculum materials and the local context work together to create new opportunities for expansive disciplinary instruction. A new metaphor: Curriculum as seed We draw on Fischer’s work (Fischer et al., 2002; Fischer 2007; 2011) from the field of Human -Computer Interaction and propose curriculum as seed as a new metaphor for the relationship between teachers, curricula, students, and the local instruct ional context (see Table 1). We draw on literature from HCI because of its focus on generating novel technological tools that can expand the potential for human functioning and learning (Bannon, 2011). In contrast to the grammar of schooling that typically  frames the curricular tools as static objects that are handed to teachers to use, HCI scholarship acknowledges that design problems in the real world require designing for open systems that end users inevitably modify and adapt to fit their needs (Fischer , 2007, 2011). The curriculum as seed  metaphor similarly acknowledges the open -ended nature of the design space; curriculum materials are viewed as starting points ( seeds) for greater innovation that materializes through the hands of teachers and students. Seeds, unlike germ cells, have a specific potentiality when they are cultivated in conditions that allow them to thrive. They also hold specific trajectories for adaptations, based on its survivability in various environmental conditions. For example, lar ge seed size is advantageous for growth under low light conditions, but, due to their weight, large seeds cannot be borne on small plants (Linkies et al., 2010). Likewise, curriculum materials embody the collective inheritance (e.g. design principles, approach to teaching and learning) that were brought forth in their creation, and yet their survivability and thriving depends on the ways in which these materials are taken up by teachers and students’ to address the needs and goals of the local environment. Teachers are local users who drive its growth and evolution because they have the agency, ownership, and knowledge that is needed to deliberately restructure, enhance, and “remix” the starting materials in ways that best address the needs and interest of their students. The curriculum as seed metaphor challenges the notion of teachers as enactors and conduits through which curricula get “delivered” into the classroom. Rather, teachers are seen as the innovators and designers, drawing on their knowledge of t heir instructional contexts, their students, and their funds of knowledge in ways that can enrich and transform the materials and the design of disciplinary instruction (Brown, 2002). This view of the teacher is directly related to the emerging scholarship  around teachers as co - designers, and the value of co-design as a valuable space for expansive learning (see Ko et al., 2022). Table 1 Curriculum as Static Object vs. Curriculum as Seed Curriculum as Static Object Curriculum as Seed Curriculum designers have all the knowledge Teachers have the knowledge and agency to creatively adapt curricula Curriculum materials are over-designed to include all possible supports for teachers and students at the outset Curriculum materials are under-designed, and become well cultivated seeds that evolve into a localized version of the curriculum that is realized in the hands of teachers and students Curriculum use remains more or less the same over time Curriculum use is dynamic and continuously evolves in response to the local context Curriculum enactment is evaluated based on alignment with original intent Curriculum enactment is used to deliberately restructure and enhance existing curricula Teachers use curriculum “as intended” by designers Teachers are informed participants who actively contribute to the evolution and creation of new curricular designs Initial curriculum = intended curriculum Initial curriculum = starting point for building new sociotechnical infrastructure within the school and/or district What does this new metaphor enable? Fischer expands on the curriculum as seed metaphor by describing the process of design and redesign as seeding, evolutionary growth, and reseeding (SER). Seeding comprises a largely planned set of activities, such as developing tasks, identifying phenomena, and embedding instructional routines into a unit. From an Indigenous perspective, seeding also involves collecting, growing, and sharing seeds to promote cultural diversity for future generations (Native American Food Sovereignty Alliance, 2022). The initial seed then undergoes evolutionary growth. During this phase, designs are treated as open systems that users can tweak, adapt, and play with. Depending on the ir effectiveness in supporting student learning, adaptations may lead to more permanent deliberate modifications and enhancements to the original materials – leading to a reseeding of new materials. To us, SER reflects a more expansive conceptualization of  curriculum design and use. Curriculum materials are positioned in the larger context of instructional systems and expected to evolve in response to the needs and desires of that system. This conceptualization also blurs the boundary between the practice o f design and the use of the curriculum materials. Instead of packaging materials to include all possible supports, curricula are intentionally open systems that teachers are expected to modify (and not “adhere to” or enact “with fidelity”). It also leaves room for – and explicitly encourages – customization and transformation. One might push back on the “idealistic” vision of curricular uptake and innovation that are implied by this metaphor, arguing that “lethal mutations” (Brown & Campione, 1996) will re sult when teachers are left to their own devices. We respond to this by arguing that a corollary of the curriculum as seed metaphor is that new forms of professional learning are required to support teachers in this complex, long -term endeavor. We need professional learning opportunities that support principled improvisation (rather than fidelity of implementation approaches) to promoting teacher learning. Teachers cannot engage with a “one size fits all” professional development (PD) in which they are walk ed through the curricular sequence and expected to adhere to the curriculum as a script. Instead, we draw on prior scholarship to conceptualize teachers’ curriculum use as one that requires principled improvisation (e.g. Philips, 2019). This metaphor creates new demands for teaching: teachers can make principled adaptations to curriculum materials (and other technological innovations) only when they understand its underlying architecture and the principles that undergird the design of lessons and the arc of  the unit (Frank & Zhao, 2003). Moreover, different approaches to PD may be needed, depending on one’s familiarity with the innovation being introduced (Frank et al., 2011). Teacher educators and PL designers will need to design opportunities to make the p urpose and curricular structure visible and invite teachers to adapt the curriculum materials in ways that align with and address the needs of their local context (Penuel et al., 2009). What counts as “curriculum” is not just the initial seed, but instead encompasses the sociotechnical infrastructures that arise to support the design and use of the modified curriculum. The resulting curricular innovation and evolution is evidence that the seed has been taken up, cultivated, and carefully nurtured. References Allen, C. D., & Heredia, S. C. (2020). Supporting High School Science Teachers in Developing Pedagogical Content Knowledge for Data Literacy Katherine Miller, Susan Yoon kmiller@gse.upenn.edu, yoonsa@upenn.edu University of Pennsylvania Abstract: As the world becomes increasingly awash in data, there is a growing need for greater focus on data literacy at the K-12 education level. Since high school teachers are often trained in one specific subject, they need additional support to teach data literacy as an integrated STEM topic. In addition to providing subject matter knowledge of data literacy, this support must focus on pedagogical content knowledge (PCK) which connects the content to the pedagogy for teaching it. As there is a major dearth of re search on PCK for data literacy this study seeks to apply existing strategies and design mechanisms that can support teachers in developing their PCK to the subject of data literacy to better understand how a new conception of PCK can be surfaced and understood. |
| A Womanist Analysis of an Asset-Based Anti-Gun Violence Curriculum | 2024 | ICLS 2024 | Kayla DesPortes, Mia Shaw, Bella Afra Boateng | Beauboeuf-Lafontant, T. (2002). A womanist experience of caring: Understanding the pedagogy of exemplary Black women teachers. The Urban Review, 34(1), 71-86. \| Kretzmann, J., & McKnight, J. P. (1996). Assets -based community development. National civic review , 85(4), 23-30. \| Thomas, E. E., & Stornaiuolo, A. (2016). Restorying the self: Bending toward textual justice. Harvard Educational Review, 86(3), 313-338. | Researchers collaborated with a social justice organization to co-design a curriculum for elementary and middle school youth to create an interactive map highlighting community assets as an exploration towards how they might combat gun violence. The curriculum was designed around Asset -Based Community Development (ABCD) to engage students in asset mapping for social action. However, the researchers noted a lack of guidance for addressing the systemic underpinnings of gun-violence. In response, the researchers applied a womanist lens— which reflects Black women and women of color’s everyday strategies for ending all forms of oppression—to examine how it might shape reflection and iteration on the curriculum. Analyzing curricular resources and codesign notes, the findings highlight the importance of fostering relationships to redistribute power, center dialogue for engaging in tensions, defining a collective communitarian approach with learners, supporting learners in storying their communities, and engaging in care and wellness as resistance. |
| Creating Thirdspace: Indigenous Learning Lab for Survivance | 2024 | ICLS 2024 | Dian Mawene, Aydin Bal | Brayboy, B. M. J. (2005). Toward a tribal critical race theory in education. The Urban Review , 37(5), 425–446. Newhope 576 DOI : 10.1007/s11256-005-0018-y | Racialization of school discipline is an enduring systemic problem in the United States. This formative intervention study is based on a collaboration with 14 school stakeholders: American Indian students, parents, community members, and educators at a rural high school in a community- driven problem-solving process called Indigenous Learning Lab (ILL). Members analyzed the root causes of the racialized school discipline and created a new behavioral support system. Utilizing critical geography and decolonizing methodology, this formative intervention study aimed to unpack how ILL members formed Thirdspace —a space of resistance, possibilities, and hopes —to address enduring racial disproportionality in school discipline. Members challenged race- neutral representations of space, mapped out dystopia, revitalized American Indian epistemology, and mapped out a real utopian vision of schooling. Racialization of school discipline in the U.S The U.S. school system has been a settler -colonial space since its inception, in which First Nations students and families struggle over cultural and linguistic erasure, recognition, and educational sovereignty (Grande, 2015; Lomawaima & McCarty, 2006; Sab zalian, 2019; Wolfe, 2006). Indigenous ontologies, epistemologies, morals, and educational practice were/are pathologized by the settler -colonial state (Lomawaima & McCarty, 2006). School discipline has historically been a tool for the settler projects of forced assimilation and cultural genocide masqueraded as “education.” American Indian youth experience multiple forms of marginalization in the settler - colonial school system. Particularly, exclusionary school discipline (e.g., out -of-school suspension, expulsion, and referrals to law enforcement) has been used as a tool of exclusion through which Indigenous bodies are erased from the learning space and white settler-colonial privilege and property are reinforced and perpetuated. Despite complexity in disproportionality patterns across states, districts, and schools, nationally American Indian students are more likely to receive frequent and harsher school discipline compared to their white counterparts (Whitford et al., 2019). Racial disproportionality is a byproduct of settler-colonial practices that spatially arrange geography of opportunity (Tate, 2008) based on racial lines and is also a tool of erasure to uphold settler -colonial power privileging whiteness over indigeneity. To disrupt the racialized school discipline system, it is imperative to investigate how power, privilege, and marginalization are indexed in local social, spatial, and historical contexts simultaneously (Soja, 2010; Tuck & McKenzie, 2015). To address settler -colonial violence embedded in disciplinary policies and practices, a formative intervention, Indigenous Learning Lab (ILL), was implemented in a close collaboration with an Anishinaabe Tribal government at Newhope High School (NHS) (Pseudonyms for all name of places and people in this paper) in a Midwest state between 2019 and 2024. As a community participatory design project, the ILL facilitated a critical design praxis of local school stakeholders —i.e., reflecting on systemic tensions a nd contradictions entrenched in the existing school system and taking collective action to co-design locally meaningful solutions in response to the needs, goals, and visions of school stakeholders ( Afacan et al., 2021; Ko et al., 2022; Ko et al., 2023). The ILL was a systemic transformation through an inclusive problem -solving space that centered  the multiple voices, perspectives, and histories of American Indian students and families, who together with school professionals designed  a new school behavioral  support system that reflected  Indigenous epistemologies and ontologies (Ko et al., 2023). This study documents how ILL members leveraged their individual and collective histories and experiences in a dialogic space to disrupt: The settler -colonial school space that is socially, historically, spatially, and politically constructed to uphold white power and privilege; and color neutral and technocratic diagnosis of and solutions to racial disparities in behavioral and academic outcomes. Through the ILL, part icipants, in particular Anishinaabe members, formed Thirdspace —a generative space filled with survivance, transgression, and new imaginations (hooks, 1989; Soja, 1996; Vizenor, 2008). 570 Theoretical framework To address racial disproportionality within social-spatial-historical contexts of settler colonialism in the Anishinaabe nation, this study was epistemologically grounded in cultural historical activity theory (CHAT) to racial disproportionality in school discipline stemming from the intellectual legacy of Vygotsky and social constructivism (1993; 1978). CHAT positions learning and human development as inseparable from the social, historical, and geographical contexts where people learn and develop as participants of multiple cultural activity systems such as family, community, and education professionals (Engeström, 2016; Rogoff, 2003). CHAT considers social, historical, and spatial underpinnings that materialize in the form of cultural tools used in disciplinary processes (e.g., a school’s behavioral expectations, rules about referral processes, personnel involved in the process, artifacts, and division of labor). Within the cultural -historical approach, racial disproportionality is a byproduct of systemic discrimination: both a result and tool that sustains settler colonial power (Lomawaima & McCarty, 2006 ). The overrepresentation of American Indian students in discipline is inseparable from the historical landscape of settler colonialism materialized in day -to-day and institutional interactions, such as the struggle over land and water, and Indigenous sovereignty. Coupled with the cultural-historical approach to racial disproportionality, this study centered Indigenous educational sovereignty through an interdisciplinary epistemological grounding informed by decolonization theory (Brayboy, 2005; Tuck & McKenzie, 2015) and critical geography (Lefebvre, 1991; Soja, 1996; Velez & Solórzano, 2017). The cross -pollination of these frameworks is powerful to investigate how settler colonialism historically produces and perpetuates racialized space through everyday schooling practices. This theoretical synthesis helped identify how Indigenous local stakeholders exercised their transformative agency to explore the new spatial possibility of resistance and sovereignty represented by the systemic design process. Thirdspace We utilized the thinking  of Henri Lefebvre (1991) and Edward Soja (1996)  to establish the basis of critical geography, specifically the concept of Thirdspace. Lefebvre's  trialectical relations of space, which encompass perceived and lived spaces, serve as a foundation for understanding of Thirdspace as a hybrid space with radical openness. Perceived space, also known as spatial practice (Lefebvre, 1991) or Firstspace (Soja, 1996), refers to the tangible, everyday environments that individuals experience through their senses, such as parks, schools, and sidewalks. These spaces are often taken for granted and shaped by social technocrats, such as urban designers and policymakers. Conceived space, also referred to as representations of space (Lefebvre, 1991) or Secondspace (Soja, 1996), encompasses the conceptual imagination of space by those in positions of power, such as architects and planners or school policy makers who design and assign space based on their expertise and knowledge. This creates orders that users of space typically accept without much resistance, as exemplified by the physical layout of schools or cities, and the proximity of reservations to educational resources. The l ived space (Lefebvre, 1991) or Thirdspace (Soja, 1996), is the mental imagination in physical space. It is the space of sense-making. The lived space encompasses the perceived and the conceived space yet simultaneously transcends them. Soja, building upon Lefebvre’s spatial trialectics, situated Lefebvre’s lived space or spaces of representation as a hybrid space (Bhabha, 1994); a gold mine of counter space where “…resistance to the dominant order arising precisely from their subordinate, peripheral or marginalized positioning” (Soja, 1996 p. 68). This counter space is what Soja termed Thirdspace. Tribal epistemology We paired the concept of Thirdspace with TribalCrit. TribalCrit recognizes the liminality of American Indian students and families as well as their historical and everyday challenges to settler -colonial practices. Resistance to settler-colonialism may create emancipatory space or the Thirdspace of sovereignt y that transcends control over lands (Bruyneel, 2007). Sovereignty is a dialectical relationship between resistance and place: “The ability of a group of people to make their own decisions and control their own lives in relation to the space where they reside and/or that they envision as their own” (Bruyneel, 2007, p. 23). The Thirdspace of sovereignty surpasses the dichotomy of colonial binary rules: Indigenous people’s location as inside or outside of the system. Given the context of the Newhope community, in this paper, we document how ILL members leveraged their individual and collective agency utilizing their lived experiences embedded in their specific socio- spatial-historical context to form Thirdspace, within which they challenged the existing settler colonial power and privilege embedded within school disciplinary policies and practices and designed a locally meaningful school level behavioral support system. 571 The present study Socio-spatial-historical contexts of Newhope High School American Indian students in NHS are overrepresented in exclusionary disciplines. In the 2019- 2020 school year in which this study occurred, NHS’s enrollment was 70.6% white, 21% American Indian, 3.2% Latine, 1% Asian, 0.1% Black or African American, and 3.7 % from two or more races (Department of Public Instruction, 2020). While American Indian students constituted one-fifth of the student enrollment, they received 64.3% of in-school suspensions and 62.8% of out-of-school suspensions (Newhope High School Discipline Data, 2019). What are visible as school discipline problems in NHS have socio- spatial-historical roots that are undetachable from Indigenous, in this case the Anishinaabe’s struggle over rights to space, ancestral land, control and exercise of treaty rights, political autonomy, economic -self-sufficiency and a desire to revive and preserve cultural heritage; struggle over recognition and control over one’s own life (Silvern, 1995). The Newhope region itself is often known as the “deep north”. In the 1980s, NHS and the city of Newhope was the epicenter of an anti- Indian movement known as the “Walleye War”, triggered by treaty rights dispute (Nesper, 2002). White protestors chanted slogans such as “ Save a walleye, spear an Indian” during protests at boat landings at lakes where the Anishinaabe spearfish. The Anishinaabe community members risked their lives fishing. The dysfunctional socio- spatial-historical climate at the community level accumulated to deep distrust and broken relationships between Natives and non-Natives at NHS. Grounded in the socio-spatial-historical context of Newhope, this paper is aimed to investigate how the ILL members centered the racialized day -to-day and institutional experiences within and outside of school in their collective efforts to create a new culturally responsive behavioral support system. |
| A Case Study of Activity Structures and Resources that Support Teacher Agency and Leadership in an RPP | 2024 | ICLS 2024 | Kelly Billings, UC Berkeley, Marcia C. Linn | None found | This case study reveals how one teacher’s agency and leadership in a Research Practice Partnership (RPP) impacted the design of a justice- centered science unit. We analyze RPP documents and data, from design meetings, workshop transcripts, observations, a nd informal co-design conversations, to reveal the ways RPP activity structures helped to support the teachers’ leadership. We report our findings in three illustrative episodes. Episode 1 reveals how the teachers’ longstanding involvement with the RPP empowered him to bring a new unit topic to the collaboration. Episode 2 documents the teachers’ leadership during workshops. Episode 3 reveals how conversations between the teacher and the learning scientist during unit implementation supported the refinement of the unit and reflection on teaching practice. |
| From Youth Participatory Art to Vision and Action: A Framework for Game-based Research-Creation | 2024 | ICLS 2024 | A Framework for Game-based Research-Creation | None found | This research explores the integration of Youth Participatory Action Research (YPAR) with game-based learning (Garcia,2020; Aleman,2023; Zhou et al., 2023) to enhance student engagement and collaboration in addressing polarised societal attention. Using a co - design methodology, students transition from passive subjects to active researchers, creating and interacting in a game-based learning environment that reflects contemporary issues such as structural oppression, privacy infringements, and environmental unsustainability. In the "Fall of Artica: A Way Back Home" (FoA) game, in the University of Toronto Schools’ Grade 11 visual art and Grade 7 English classes, students work in groups to tackle wicked problems in a novel dystopian narrative. Through collective inquiry in diverse social contexts, students develop new narratives and visual assets, foster collective inquiry and critical reflections in visual and literary art creations, and form a culture of community and practice. Through several rounds of enhancement of this game-based curriculum, we have articulated a YPAR framework that could potentially advance FoA and other serious game -based research regarding student engagement beyond participation in gameplay, co-design, co-contribution, and content creation. Rationale Empowering Youth through Game -based Participatory Action Research offers a novel approach to engaging students actively in co -creating knowledge. This research explores the impact of game -based learning environments on critical engagement in collaboration that challenges their ability to confront polarizing views and beliefs. The current cultural production landscape, marked by increased digital activism and the necessity for critical literacy (Amgott, 2018), sets the stage for this investigation. We also a ddress the ethical dilemma of data ownership and its benefits in youth research (Brown & Campione, 1996), exploring how games can facilitate changes in power structures and foster collaborative, active participation in communicative action (Habermas,1990). Theoretical perspective Youth Participatory Action Research (YPAR) embodies a transformative and collaborative approach where youth actively address complex societal issues, aligning closely with critical literacy and digital activism (Amgott, 2018). With some roots in the broader methodology of design- based research Brown (1992), YPAR fosters dynamic and participatory learning environments, interweaving the principles of critical pedagogy (Freire, 2020) to help prepare students for the challenges of the 21st century. It incorporates Gee’s (2005) concept of semiotic social spaces, adding depth to contemporary social -cultural contexts and power dynamics. We also seek to establish and scaffold a knowledge community (Raman. et al.,2021; Peters & Slotta, 2010), emphasizing collaborative, asynchronous learning through a dystopian game, Fall of Artica (Zhou et al., 2023).  Fall of Artica (FoA) values diversity in cultural backgrounds (Quintero, 2009; Sahni, 2001), advocating for a collective and participatory learning approach. Integrating insights on cross -context curriculum design (Stille & Prasad, 2015; Souto-Manning, 2008) and drawing on Squire’s (2006) perspective on game -based learning spaces, YPAR promotes active, critical, and creative engagement with pressing issues in their commu nities, equipping youth with resilience and the skills needed to navigate, respond to and even thrive in this complex, unsettling, and imperfect contemporary world. Research questions 1) How does a game-based youth participatory action research framework influence student engagement, critical thinking, and collaboration in responding to systemic issues?  2) How does a game -based learning environment with a dystopian theme affect students’ awareness and inquiry of systemic problems in the real world? 3) How do a game-based narrative and interactions, such as collaborative storytelling and multimodal expression, contribute to students’ critical inquiry and resolution on social conflict and community dissensus? 1782 |
| Designing Participatory Teacher Learning Environments: Inquiries | 2024 | ICLS 2024 | Methods, and Perspectives from Learning Sciences | Cochran-Smith, M., & Lytle, S. L. (1999). Relationships of knowledge and practice: Teacher learning in co mmunities. Review of Research in Education, 24, 249. \| Goodwin, C. (2018). Co-operative action. Cambridge University Press. Gutiérrez, K. D., & Vossoughi, S. (2010). Lifting off the ground to return anew: Mediated praxis, transformative learning, and social design experiments. Journal of Teacher Education, 61(1– 2), 100–117. Gutiérrez, K. D. (2014). Integrative research review: Syncretic approaches to literacy learning. leveraging horizontal knowledge and expertise. In P. Dunston, L. Gambrell, K. Headley, S. Fullerton, & P. Stecker (Eds.), 63rd Literacy Research Association yearbook (pp. 48–61). Altamonte Springs, FL. Gutiérrez, K. D., Engeström, Y., & Sannino, A. (2016). Expanding educational research and interventionist Methodologies. Cognition and Instruction, 34(3), 275–284. Gutiérrez, K. D., & Jurow, A. S. (2016). Social design experiments: Toward equity by design. Journal of the Learning Sciences, 25(4), 565–598. | Teacher educators seek approaches to design teacher learning environments that empower teachers to address situated problems of teaching by shaping their own learning. This symposium provides a learning sciences perspective on these efforts, sharing insights from six studies for designing and examining participatory teacher learning environments (PTLEs). The lessons derived provide an initial framework for designing PTLEs, highlighting three shared features: creating alternative forms of relationality, drawing on histories for transformation, and engaging in collective future-making for agentic teacher learning. Symposium objective Teacher educators have increasingly recognized that teaching problems are socially embedded, deeply situated, and complex (Horn & Garner, 2022). This points to the need to design more participatory and flexible teacher learning environments, offering teachers space, time, and support to reflect on and make sense of their problems of practices, while agentically engaging their professional learning and problem solving processes through multiple resources, such as scientific knowledge in their field, their pr ofessional experiences, and relationships. Designing such teacher learning environments requires a dialogic approach between research- based knowledge and practice-based knowledge on teaching rather than the usual hierarchy between these, which is not an easy endeavor for teacher educators (Cochran -Smith & Lytle, 1999). However, our field, the learning sciences, provides theoretically robust perspectives and methods to contribute to these efforts and to study the learning processes within what we call participatory teacher learning environments (PTLEs). To illustrate with just a few of these precedents, we look to co- design partnerships (Penuel et al., 2007) and participatory design research (Gutiérrez et al., 2016), where the latter refers to a diverse range of approaches, all drawing on Freirean critical pedagogy and/or Vygotskian socio-cultural theories of learning (Freire, 2000; Vygotsky, 1997). Independently of the learning sciences, some teacher education projects have developed their own methods, having many intersections with these approaches. To consolidate these approaches, this symposium brings together research conducted by learning scientists who have designed PTLEs. Each paper discusses an aspect of their designs that invites teachers to participate in their own learning. During the symposium, we seek to generate insights for the design and implementation of PTLEs more broadly, as well as approaches to analysis of the learning processes within them. Issues highlighted in this collection of papers help us to derive some initial lessons in the design of PTLEs. First, the fundamental element of a PTLE is relationality that counters traditional hierarchical role distributions in teacher learning.  All papers provide us insights about this aspect, while Papers 3, 5, and 6 specifically shed light on this issue. Esboldt (Paper 3) brings an important concept, politicized trust (Vakil & McKinney de Royston, 2019), into our attention in coach-teacher relations, uncovering its influence in giving rise to very different enactments of standardized programs. Aldemir et al. (Paper 5) extend the meaning of "participation" in PTLEs by focusing on how student participation in co-design partnerships helps teachers reflect on their traditional roles in teacher -student relationships and experience transformative teacher learning toward imagining new teacher roles for more democratic classrooms. Chen (Paper 6) makes the case that researchers’ deep reflection on their own emotions, experiences, and perspectives is valuable in transforming teacher - researcher relations to achieve PTLEs. 1910 Sec ond, PTLEs provide teachers with tools, space, and time to reflect on their current problems, questions, and extant understandings and use their own history as a resource for transformative and agentic learning. Three papers specifically highlight this aspect of their designs. Shapiro et al. (Paper 1) focus on how a dynamic visualization tool supports teachers in unraveling and bringing their own questions to the PTLE space. In Harmon (Paper 4), we see how the personal and collective histories of Black women become a tool for them to reflect on their current teaching ideologies. Günal-Aggül and Öner (Paper 2) discuss how teachers found space to draw on their histories in collectively constructing teaching concepts in novel ways. Any learning activity is future-oriented, aiming for change in perspectives and practices. However, who designs and owns this future is mostly ignored in controlled learning spaces. Alternatively, as the third feature, PTLEs are spaces in which teachers are agents in designing the future of their practices in dialogue with their colleagues, researchers/teacher educators, and students while collectively working toward creating more equitable and meaningful learning environments for students. To become a PTLE, teachers need to experience the second and third features together in achieving praxis (Freire, 2000). In Shapiro et al., along with reflection on their histories, we see how the PTLE design supported teachers to transform their classroom space and control their own learning process. Günal -Aggül and Öner show how teacher educators can draw on the method of formative interventions  (Engeström, 2011) and the concept of third space  (Gutiérrez, 2014) while supporting teachers’ collective future- making. Harmon exemplifies how Black woman pre -service teachers found space to be agents of transformation for equity instead of objects of ‘diversity’ projects designed by others. During the 60-minute symposium, each author will give a ~7 min individual presentations. For the last 20-minute, we will facilitate a group discussion. Paper 1: Supporting teachers’ agentic sensemaking and video-based formative feedback: An interaction geography perspective Ben Rydal Shapiro, Elizabeth Metts, Yeliz Günal Aggül and Ilana Horn Objectives As teacher educators have increasingly acknowledged the situated and complex nature of teaching, teacher agency gained more attention as a major outcome in meaning -centered teacher learning environments (Horn & Garner, 2022). Whereas traditional teacher learning environments constrain teachers’ agency through static and predetermined learning processes and outcomes, recent work is exploring environments that use new methods to empower teachers’ agentic sensemaking. Informed by this work, we have been developing a video- based formative feedback (VFF) cycle in a participatory design research project. In this paper, we explore how a method called interaction geography supports this process and teachers’ agentic sensemaking  by providing ways to dynamically visualize classroom video data (Shapiro et al., 2024 https://www.interactiongeography.org) Theoretical framework To operationalize agency, we draw on the notion of mediated praxis (Freire, 2000; Gutiérrez & Vossoughi, 2010; Vygotsky, 1997). We define teacher agency as teachers’ mediated reflection and action to control their professional learning and problem-solving processes by employing social, material, and conceptual resources. To support teachers’ agentic sensemaking, learning environments need to provide space and support so that teachers can connect the past to the future of their activities, empowering them to become historical actors in shaping their future practices (Gutiérrez et al., 2016). Based on this perspective, we explore how a dynamic visualization tool called the interaction geography slicer (IGS) mediated teachers’ praxis within the VFF . Data sources and methods As a part of a larger research practice partnership with a professional development organization supporting experienced mathematics teachers’ learning around collaborative student groupwork, we conducted VFF cycles with 12 participating teachers focusing o n groupwork monitoring practices (VFF -M). VFF-M cycles centered teachers’ questions of interest around their monitoring work. We collected and analyzed detailed video lesson data with the teacher’s question in mind. Finally, we facilitated a collaborative, reflective debrief with the teacher and colleagues using the IGS to view and discuss the teacher’s questions alongside their movement, conversation, and video clips (see IGS visualization at: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=wIyVYCuPxl0). Results Through a grounded, thematic analysis (Glaser & Strauss, 1967) of VFF-M debriefs across the year, we identified several themes illustrating ways the IGS supported teachers’ agentic sensemaking. First, the IGS served as a 1911 medi ating tool for teachers’ praxis in reflecting on their use of classroom space, making sense of their current problems and dilemmas, envisioning alternative ways of designing space, and transforming their classroom space to more closely align with their  pedagogical commitments. Second, the IGS encouraged teachers’ exploratory analysis and questioning to support co- inquiry into teachers’ identified questions, while triggering them to ask more questions. Finally, teachers were not only enthusiastic about the possibilities of incorporating this tool into their reflections on practice, but actively co -designed new features they desired, including ways of coding movement, visualizing conversation, and describing patterns of movement and space. Significance Our findings emphasize how integrating interaction geography within the VFF -M process offered new ways not only for teachers to reflect on spatial and mobile dimensions of their teaching practice, but empowered them to critically discuss and advance ways t hey designed their classroom spaces and used their movement and physical space to support student learning. More generally, this work highlights the value and limitations of dynamic visualization tools such as the IGS when integrated with video-based reflection processes to develop more agentic participatory teacher learning environments in the future. Paper 2: Putting everyday and scientific knowledge into dialogue: Teachers’ concept construction in a participatory teacher learning environment Yeliz Günal Aggül and Diler Öner Objectives Research on teacher communities has revealed that teachers’ concept development is complex, reflecting the tensions between the teachers’ situated needs and scientific knowledge of teaching, especially when the latter are presented as fixed (Horn & Kane, 2 019). This study examines how the learning practices in a PTLE designed to support a group of teachers in designing their professional learning community help to overcome this problem. Design and theoretical framework To support teachers in creating their community, we designed a PTLE following the method of formative interventions (Engeström, 2011). In formative interventions, researchers support practitioners in producing novel solutions to their situated problems thr ough a series of sequential activities organized according to Engeström’s (2016) theory of expansive learning. The seven expansive learning actions are questioning and analyzing problems in an activity, modeling, examining, and implementing a novel solution, reflecting  on the process, and consolidating the solution. We designed the PTLE accordingly and supported the participants in modeling their community as a solution to the problems they identified. Here, focusing on the modeling action, we will examine how teachers’ everyday experience and scientific knowledge interacted with each other in third spaces (Gutiérrez, 2014) as they were discussing and defining the concepts of teaching as a part of the community building process. |
| Redesigning Tabletop Games as a Culturally Sustaining Pedagogy in Canadian Middle School Classrooms | 2024 | ICLS 2024 | Beaumie Kim, Reyhaneh Bastani, Bogusia Gierus, Jerremie Clyde, Alexandria Poppendorf | None found | We present our study of middle school students in Canada exploring tabletop game redesign as a culturally sustaining pedagogy. The teachers and the researchers co- designed the activities and data was collected on the students’ engagement with the activities and each other. We describe the design of activities and preliminary findings. Our project is drawn from research on how learners engage creatively and critically with disciplines when playing and designing games, considering how tabletop games are played across generations and reflect socio- cultural contexts and world     views. Tabletop games’ use of physical objects encourages communication beyond verbal or textual forms that suit players of different ages and linguistic backgrounds (Kim et al., 2023). We present our study of how tabletop game redesign could help middle school learners with varying cultural backgrounds might see individual differences as resources for social learning (Ladson-Billings, 1995). Cultural practices and tabletop game redesign Tabletop games are and have been culturally significant (Crist et al., 2016) and have often been adopted across cultures. In our past work on mathematics learning, we suggested that redesigning activities could enrich linguistic and cultural diversity (Kim et al., 2023) by allowing students to create their own ways of learning (Civil, 2002). By redesigning, students collaborated with a common and deep understanding of the original game and its rules. Students adopted rules of their cultural games and used their origins’ languages with friends. This process embodies the essence of culturally sustain ing pedagogies, i.e., the pursuit of cultural and linguistic pluralism (Paris, 2012). We see cultures as meanings and practices inherited and formed individually and collectively in social groups and believe that learners could bring in diverse cultural resources by our approach. Figure 1 Tabletop Game Redesign Process Model Research design The researchers worked with teachers at a culturally and linguistically diverse Canadian urban middle school. We started with co-design workshops in September 2022. To facilitate student learning and our data collection, we created a model (see Figure 1) and a student workbook to support the redesign process based on our previous work (Jaques et al., 2019). We used Forbidden Island ® as a suitably cooperative game with simple rules. The co- design workshops were focused on exploring the ways of thinking and learning in game redesign and how to integrate it with the teachers’ current teaching. We collected data from two Grade 7 classes during both Humanities and Science/Mathematics from December 2022 to April 2023. We explored how the game redesign approach was implemented and taken up by both the students and teachers in this project. Students were assigned to groups by the teachers and then we selected groups for data collection based on the group members’ informed consent. To maintain the anonymity of those students who had given consent, researchers kept the appearance of collecting data across all groups. At least one researcher visited 2163 their classes as a participant observes. Students documented their ideas in workbooks, and the researchers took photos and videos of their in- progress designs. Students showcased their final game design to their families and peers in a game night event. After this event, we conducted group interviews with students. Tabletop game redesign and culturally sustaining learning In the ongoing analysis we are engaged in thematic analysis to review our notes, classroom recordings, and learner artifacts (i.e., games, photos, workbooks). Our analysis aims to identify how students construct ways to draw and connect with their interests, cultural practices and experiences, and personal stories . We are also investigating how redesign ideas formed and evolved, and how we could better support this process. In this poster presentation, we will focus on three major themes that have been identified thus far. Slippage of multiple selves of students. Our findings show that students expressed their personal interests (e.g., game characters, their country of origin) and engaged in conversations related to their cultural practices (e.g., women’s roles in their households) and stories from their extended families (e.g., an uncle’s difficult journey as a refugee), regardless of such conversations directly impacting game design. This slippage showed us a glimpse of their cultures, interests, and relationships within and beyond the school boundaries as they became part of their school life. One of the students, for example, was constantly at the boundary of being a fun friend and a serious student. He was playful with teammates, playing with toys and talking about video games, while he would talk in a mature way with the researchers and the teachers about their game designs. Inventiveness of students’ redesign process. Some students found completing the redesign workbook a source of disengagement rather than a tool for brainstorming. Other students still engaged in brainstorming while diligently filling it out, while others came up with new methods of articulating their ideas, reflecting their interest in media production. For example, two students in a group started recording videos of themselves interviewing each other about their game design ideas while another group pretended doing a YouTube instruction video for their game looking at our camera. The role of the “intruders.” Our observations indicate that teachers or researchers’ uninvited visits could interrupt activities (regardless of their legitimacy). The classes had similar structures, but what happened after their visits were starkly different depending on the adult’s pedagogical approach. When the students were deeply engaged in discussions of their ideas, the teacher/researcher could interrupt their flow, regardless of their intentions. On the other hand, an intervention from the same goal -oriented adult helped other students who were not engaged to find new directions for their design. After interacting with these adults who focused on listening, students often continued with their previous process and found new insights to improve their ideas. Discussion and conclusions We are exploring how students invent ways to draw on cultural practices and differences while redesigning tabletop games. Individual students negotiate redesign ideas and learning topics while making sense of their own and each other’s interests, culture, and personalities. The preliminary analysis showed what might be difficult for teachers to know, such as students’ conversations related to their cultural practices, and what happens when teachers and researchers introduce the ideas deemed helpful to students. As participant observers, researchers’ presence and occasional visits to groups’ work could also influence learners’ design and thinking. Notably, researchers’ design involvement is a significant responsibility – we cherish the slippages that occur in students’ chatter that may not find ways into their design, and their invention of new ways of learning, thinking, and designing. At the same time, we continue to design new structures to help students learn and engage with decades of established public education. Striking this delicate balance is an important agenda for our on -going work. References Civil, M. (2002). Everyday mathematics, mathematicians’ mathematics, and school mathematics: Can we bring them together? Journal for Research in Mathematics Education. Monograph, 11, 40–62. Crist, W., de Voogt, A., & Dunn -Vaturi, A. E. (2016). Facilitating interaction: Board games as social lubricants in the ancient near east. Oxford Journal of Archaeology, 35(2), 179–196. Early Childhood Education, 26(1), 22–29. Kim, B., Bastani, R., & Takeuchi, M. A. (2023). Embodied mathematical practices in (re)designing board games in a linguistically diverse classroom.
