| Exploring the Intersection of Emotions and Epistemic Engagement | 2025 | ICLS 2025 | Jooeun Shim, Weiyi Zhang, Vivian Leung, Zoe Nguyen, Tiina Törmänen, Hanna Järvenoja, Chew Lee Teo, Carol Chan, Aloysius Ong, Trang C. Tran, Joseph L. Polman | Philip, T. M., & Gupta, A. (2020). Emerging perspectives on the co- construction of power and learning in the learning sciences, mathematics education, and science education. Review of Research in Education, 44(1), 195-217. | This symposium explores the complex relationship between emotions and epistemic engagement across diverse learning contexts. Emotions, integral to cognitive processes, can enhance epistemic engagement by triggering curiosity, focus, and critical evaluation, especially when knowledge -building is the goal. This session will present studies on the relationships between emotions and epistemic cognition, within different thematic areas, such as social media interactions, bioinformatics education, data-infused interdisciplinary learning, and knowledge- building collaborative online platforms. Each presentation aims to uncover the roles emotions play in fostering or inhibiting epistemic actions. The discussant will synthesize insights from these studies to shed light on the generative potent ial of emotions in supporting critical and reflective engagement. The o utcomes of this symposium can deepen our  understanding of the role emotions can play in fostering epistemic engagement in formal and informal settings. Symposium overview Emotions have been argued to play an integral role in guiding rational thinking and decision making (da So usa, 2009). Although emotions are a well -studied area of research, significant gaps remain in understanding how emotions relate to epistemic engagement and learning. Most research on emotions in education has focused on the type s (i.e., achievement emotions) and valence (i.e., positive/negative) of emotions  (Pekrun & Linnenbrink- Garcia, 2012), emotional regulation, the influence of the setting on emotional responses  (Järvenoja et al., 2009), and teacher-student interactions and emotions. Few studies report on the links between emotions and epistemic engagement (Muis et al., 2015). Emotions comprise interrelated psychological processes that occur in response to an event that is subjectively important; these processes can have affective, cognitive, physiological, motivational, and expressive components (Scherer, 2009). They refer to conscious emotional experiences which are distinguished according to their object focus and according to two dimensions: activation and valence (Scherer, 2009). Pekrun and Stephens (2012) distinguish four types of academic emotions according to their object focus: achievement, epistemic, topic emotions, and social emotions. When knowledge generation is the object that the emotional reaction is focused on, people experience epistemic emotions  (Pekrun & Linnenbrink-Garcia, 2012); these emotions are caused by the cognitive qualities of an information task and the processing required to complete it (Pekrun & Linnenbrink- Garcia, 2012).  Some of the commonly mentioned emotions that may arise during information processing are surprise, joy, fear, anxiety, anger, frustration and curiosity (Muis et al., 2018). Epist emic engagement has cognitive, behavioral and affective components, and takes into account the goals or aims one sets across different information contexts, and the criteria and strategies relied on  in order to reach epistemic judgments  (Varda et al., 2024 ). It can foster epistemic actions, which can lead to acquiring or generating knowledge, or gaining enhanced understanding. Emotions can arise as a result of goal-directed 2287 cognitive activity  but can also influence next steps in the cognitive activity ( D’Mello & Graesser, 2012). An emotional response is considered a precursor to cognitive, motivational behavioral, and social engagement in learning (Pekrun & Linnenbrink-Garcia, 2012) and can serve different epistemic functions according to the phase of learning and engagement. For instance, emotions can provide salience by focusing attention on distinct aspects of a situation and  can also function as an additional source of knowledge , and, in turn, both of these epistemic functions can contribute to cognitive efficiency. This symposium will explore links between emotions and epistemic engagement by presenting research examining different learning contexts, thematic contexts, and methodologies :  Yoon, Shim , Zhang, Leung and Nguyen will discuss how socioscientific issues in high school science can foster strong emotions that can lead to generative epistemic practices. They investigate student interactions with bioinformatics curricula that require them to collect and analyze empirical air -quality data in local neig hborhoods and demonstrate the prevalence of curiosity, surprise, and enjoyment that propel further epistemic engagement. Törmänen and Järvenoja will report findings from a study investigating how secondary school students in collaborative groups respond emotionally and regulate their learning when encountering cognitive and metacognitive challenges, termed as epistemic challenges. Teo, Chan, and Ong will report on the investigation of students’ academic emotions when dealing with epistemic-oriented ideas during online collaborative knowledge building using Knowledge Forum. Tran and Polman will contextualize the emotionally -laden process of learning about and writing a data story on the experience of Japanese Americans during forced incarceration in World War II, drawing from data from a broader co-design project aimed at integrating data p ractices into interdisciplinary learning. Their focus will be on discussing the intricate and complex relationship of the interplay between emotion, sensemaking, and practice. Kyza and Varda will discuss social media as an informal space where emotional reactions to controversial posts may spur information; their focus is on characterizing emotional reactions and examining their co-occurrence and relation to different types of epistemic engagement. Their aim is to understand the extent to which, and how, adults engage in critical evaluation of information shared on social media.   Tabak, Reznick and Radinsky will present data that emotion plays a role in how people form epistemic trust relationships in naturalistic settings. They call for increased recognition that forming epistemic trust extends beyond analytic appraisals. They demonstrate these ideas through adult retrospective interviews concerning their health information behavior. David Hammer, who has written extensively on the topic of students’ epistemologies and on the relation between emotion and cognition, will be the discussant of this symposium.  The symposium will consist of a 5- minute introduction which will address the overarching questions that the symposium seeks to answer, followed by individual presentations (10-min each) and the commentary by the Discussant (10 -min). This will allow for a 15-minute general discussion between the symposium participants and the session attendees. The motivation behind this more traditional format is to allow everyone to form a deeper understanding of each team’s research, |
| Centering Community-Rooted Actions of Hope: Beyond Knowledge Monoculture | 2025 | ICLS 2025 | Miwa Aoki Takeuchi, Deborah Dutta, Fernanda Coelho Liberali, Tyrell Anderson, Racquel Banazsak, Michael Bakal, Voces y Manos, Michael Cole, Moises Esteban-Guitart, Institut de Recerca Educativa, Lisa Ghost, Kris Gutiérrez, Shakhnoza Kayumova, the Un ited States, Erma Mujic, Preeti Patil, Founder of Urban Leaves, Antti Rajala, Université de Neuchâtel, George Spears, Darci Ann Sigana | Marin, A., & Bang, M. (2018). “Look it, this is how you know:” Family forest walks as a context for knowledge- building about the natural world. Cognition and Instruction, 36(2), 89–118. Meixi, & Nzinga, K. (2024). Origin theories of learning: A river with many sources. Review of Research in Education, 47(1), 474–535. | Our sym posium is a confluence of researchers/pedagogues/designers /practitioners who embrace porousness of these identity categories, toward collectively questioning and challenging knowledge and environmental monoculture (Shiva, 1993). We recognize the resurgence of endangered ways of knowing and ways of living as a necessary intervention to environmental destruction and coloniality. While facing the overwhelming devastation of environmental degradation and repercussions from globalization and col onialism, we refuse to cede with ontological negation (Bang, 2020). Instead, we together seek to ground ourselves in community-rooted praxis and pedagogy of hope ( Freire, 1992; Gutiérrez, 2016). We collectively present locally and globally relevant socio- environmental issues that arose from communities beyond academic siloes. Together, we highlight community-based actions of hope representing diverse geographical localities and histories. Collectively, we gesture toward knowledge polyculture that is life affirming and orients us toward plural alternative visions of multi-species flourishing. Framing our dialogue: Stewarding plural multi-species flourishing In problematizing knowledge production systems that force Indigenous activists and their communities to talk back to and talk up to power, Smith (2012) argued ethics and integrity in telling the story right and well. This provocation touches upon ethics and values embedded in subject-subject relations or relationships between social actors in research, but also encompasses into subject-object relations that need to be fundamentally scrutinized in participatory design research (Bang & Vossoughi, 2016). Without critical examination on “what forms of knowledge are generated, how, why, where, and by whom” (Bang & Vossoughi, 2016, p. 174), researchers in the learning sciences might risk perpetuating normative hierarchy of power. Pointing out the violence embedded in globalized, hegemonic forms of knowledge acquisition, Visvanathan (2009) calls for cognitive justice in recognizing that knowledges go beyond purely epistemic but are embedded in ecologies, life-worlds, cosmologies and livelihoods. Taking seriously the diverse ways of knowing, in their vital, dialogic form (as opposed to curated and extractive abstractions) engenders democratic and equitable forms of inquiry. Movements toward cognitive justice share visions for epistemic justice that seek to redress marginalization and oppression through testimonial credibility, access to information, and the availability of proper representation (Fricker, 2013; Penuel & Watkins, 2019). Yet, gesturing toward cognitive justice has a distinct orientation to interrogating the repercussions of globalization, histories of colonization, and environmental destruction, which resulted from and resulted in endangering the lived knowledge of the most vulnerable, being entangled with ecology and plural ways to live. In this context, our symposium gathers  researchers/pedagogues/activists/designers who share concerns for locally and globally relevant socio-environmental justice issues, while representing diversity in geographical, geopolitical locality and histories. We all take the question of telling the story right and well seriously, and engage 2318 in constant reflection on ethics, responsibility, and morals in research-based storytelling toward what Visvanathan (2009) envisioned as cognitive justice. We share visions for social design experiments rooted in “a  place of hope and possibility, stemming out of the material reality that there are many groups of people who have been, an d continue to be, oppressed as a result of systemic injustice” (Gutiérrez et al., 2020, p. 332). We contemplate on the forms of knowledge creation that could mediate the process of participants becoming designers of their own futures, especially in the context of socio -environmental justice issues at stake. While facing the overwhelming devastation of environmental degradation and the spread of “seeds that have been modified through settler - colonialism,” we refuse “the ceding of ontological ground to negations” (Bang, 2020, p. 438). Instead, we together seek to ground our work in the “grammar of hope, possibility, and resilience” (Gutiérrez, 2016, p. 188). We embrace our collective hope with decolonial love (Betasamosake Simpson, 2013)— “ love that resists the nature- culture or nature-society divide, love to know our responsibilities and enact them in ways that give back, and love that does not neglect historical oppression and violence yet carries us through” ( Takeuchi & Marin, 2022, p.1). Countering epistemic hegemony entails critically questioning the dominant paradigms of reason, growth and development that have come to define the standards and modes of learning and living. The closely coupled system of capitalistic exploitation and reductive, disembodied techno-scientific rationality has tried to build socio- cultural narratives that value parameters such as profit, scale, uniformity and efficiency over ideas of reciprocity, contextual appropriateness, relationality and interdependence (Date et al., 2021). Rooted in pioneering environmental activism work in India, Vandana Shiva (1993) problematized “monocultures of the mind,” the ways in which modern agri-science with its focus on narrow conceptions of yields of a few staple grains erased and delegitimised Indigenous knowledge systems based on soil ecology, plant health, pest -predator relationships and seed sovereignty. Shiva (1993) has warned  that monocultures of the mind would result in environmental degradation, depleted biodiversity, impoverished diets and food apartheid, and loss of livelihoods, all of which affect the most vulnerable in globalized systems of power. The modern myth of productivity to stall scarcity is built on assumptions of competition and control. In Epistemologies of the South, Boaventura de Sousa Santos (2015) refers to such systems of thought as lazy reasoning, explaining that deterministic, dualistic, the supposed infallibility of progress -based arguments only serves to highlight the arrogance and ultimate ignorance underlying the dominant narrative. Moving away from these self-destructive and life -impoverishing socio-technical monocultures requires nurturing diverse ecologies of thought and mediums of knowledge  beyond coloniality (de Sousa Santos, 2015; Mignolo, 2007). Bringing to fore the  marginalized counterfactual knowledges and lived experiences demands imagination and courage to explore the range of possibilities existing in community-oriented praxis of resistance and hope (Freire, 1992). Our symposium is an invitation to broaden these possibilities  and steward the ecosystems that allow for multiple alternative visions of multi- species flourishing and well -being to manifest. Toward this, we seek reflections from people working deeply in communit ies, are mindful of coloniality and cultivating decolonial present/future, and have been taking community-rooted actions aligned with cognitive justice. Many of us in this symposium spend hours grounding ourselves in community, engage in dialogues with multiple stakeholders, and create mediums of communication beyond conventional academic publications. Our symposium brings together heterogeneous voices of an intergenerational group of scholars and community-based or school -based practitioners. Our symposium is an orchestration of  voices rooted in community and highlighting diversity in geographical, geopolitical localities and histories. With curated hybrid engagement spaces, we invite voices from teachers, community workers, families , and researchers. Each group of presenters in this symposium will share their work using the following guiding questions: 1) What is the context in which you are working? 2) In what ways, your work disrupts a particular monoculture of knowledge system? In other words, what issue prompted your specific research/educational engagement? 3) What forms of learning your work has tried to center and nurture? 4) How do you see your work contributing to community-rooted actions of hope? Situated in the urban context of India, Dutta, Patil , and Mukherjee together cultivate hope (Dutta & Hazra, 2023), for community-based environmental stewardship to rewrite the images of Mumbai as a metropolitan space for human -centered activities. Kayumova shares her longitudinal project that positions racia lized multilingual youth at the center of radical possibilities for reimagining social, cultural and environmental futures rooted in care, relationality, and interdependence —a pluriverse of STEM discipline (Kayumova & Dou, 2022). In the context of Indigenous -led public schools in Mní Sóta Makhóčhe and Storywalks (Marin & Bang, 2018), Meixi, Banazsak, Spears, Sigana, Ghost, Anderson, and Mujic together illuminate how the Land teaches us about how we live relationally (Meixi & Nzinga, 2024; Wildcat, 2013 ). Drawing from their community -engaged co- design efforts in the Maya-Achí community, Bakal and Gutiérrez leverage a telos of Buen Vivir (Bakal & Reyes, 2023) as a key principle for social design-based experiment (Gutiérrez et al., 2020) toward a utopian future. From 2319 the praxis of utopian methodologies ( Rajala, Cole, & Esteban -Guitart, 2023) and highlighting non- linearity and heterochronicity, Rajala, Cole, and Esteban-Guitart illustrate tangible utopias in rural and urban schools in Finland through sustainable futures projects enacted by teachers, and in collaboration with the municipal government . Following co-chair’s framing (8 minutes), each group will present their work in 12 minutes each (a total of 60 minutes), followed by synthesizing commentary from our discussant and conversations with the audiences. Liberali’s commentary as a discussant draws from her longitudinal praxis rooted in critical, counter-colonial, and history-making imaginaries. Liberali’s theoretical works are rooted in her designed practices and also lived experiences situated in Rio de Janeiro and São Paulo  (Liberali, 2019) , re flecting languages, diversities, and education to redress socio-environmental and socio-economical injustices. Her remarks will deepen our collective visions for fostering alternative knowledge systems that are not hegemonic and colonial. Our hybrid symposium will create a space that hosts each team’s stories and related academic and public works, which can be a resource for sustained discussion. Collectively, our symposium intends to nurture sustained dialogues including collective sharing of unfinished thoughts and questions, with the Learning Sciences community. Cultivating hope: Reflections on community urban gardening experiences in India to reimagine human-nature relationships in cities Deborah Dutta, Preeti Patil, Samira Mukherjee Studies estimate nearly 60 percent of the population will be living  in urban areas by 2050 (Cohen, 2006). The growth of cities is especially noticeable in the Global South, with an aggressive expansion of urban areas. The ‘development’ trajectories employed in  the creation of urban spaces largely borrow from the Global North and capital-intensive, industrial narratives, thus relying on pipeline models of resource extraction (Shiva, 1993). The design of cities has been critiqued by practitioners and scholars for creating a “ metabolic rift” as part of its exploitative processes of sustaining itself at the expense of other ecosystems (Dutta, 2024). As myriad forms of socio-ecological crises proliferate, with cities being responsible for a significant amount of carbon emissions, waste production and material consumption),  there is an urgent need to reimagine our relationship, roles, and place in cities. The issue is of special significance to the field of learning sciences, given the increasing scholarship on embodied and emplaced dimensions of learning, which also form the basis for socio-political action (Takeuchi & Marin, 2022). Critical attention to the geographical and socio-ecological infrastructure of urban spaces thus has implications for ways in which the situated, embodied and cultural aspects of learning and living are envisioned. In our projects, we focused on urban food systems as a domain of practice and reflection. The socio -economic arrangements and often opaque supply chains in cities are entangled with questions of food security and nutrition in fundamental ways. As Frazier (2018) explains, “Food is a productive site for exploring the intimate intersection of bodies and cities. This is in part due to the inextricable relationship between food, the body, and place. As scholars have long identified, food is a symbolic and material force that transcends and delimits particular places and communities” (p.12). Linking the act of consuming food to the conditions under which food is grown and brought to our plates requires an empathetic, systemic way of thinking about the human –nature relationship. The context and methodology Our projects explored community-based stewardship or “civic ecology” practices (Krasny & Tidball, 2012) based on the principle of humans nested within larger socio-ecological systems and being able to impact the systems in positive ways through feedback and virtuous cycles of empowerment. Drawing on Social Design- Experiments approach (Gutiérrez, 2016), our efforts call for attention to the dynamic nature of cultural interactions with food, soil, and plants and how these mediate transformative trajectories for individuals and communities. The approach focuses on resilience stemming from emergent, systemic changes within the community. Our collective experiences of volunteering at an urban community garden, facilitating a school-terrace garden and collaborating with community partners served to highlight the evolving knowledge and perspectives that grew along with the spaces, as they transformed into alternate visions of cityscapes ranging from local campaigns to save native seeds, petition against GMO crops, and a renewed interest in traditional farming practices adapted for urban spaces. Findings and contributions Together, these initiatives illustrate that i) feedback and competency arising from sustained interaction with artefacts in community -based practices motivate individual sphere of actions, which can then extend into the wider community, ii) ecological perspectives are embedded in 'performative' substances such as compost, native seeds, manure etc as interacting with these entities allowed growers to understand the embedded views towards nature in an embodied (i.e. non- descriptive) way, while also recreating  in an urban setting agricultural practices 2320 that are rapidly disappearing from India’s villages.  iii) embodied encounters are central to developing care-based relationships with the environment, and iv) shared- practices support extending ecological actions to the wider community. Many participants reported developing pro- environmental values such as frugality, re -usability and promoting biodiversity, which emerged through sustained participation in group activities that embed these principles. This indicates that ecological practices such as community farming are a way to help implicitly develop an alternative value system towards the environment, and generate the rich, moral imagination necessary to think and act in ways to promote multispecies flourishing (as seen in our illustrated narratives, Dutta & Hazra, 2023). Given the atrophied and opaque nature of socio -ecological relationships in cities, facilitating such rich experiences, and understanding the challenges of  acting upon them offer a promising trajectory for research in learning sciences. These initiatives also question the development narratives and urban architecture usually taken for granted in city planning by actively positioning alternate visions for just, sustainable futures. Stories and visions of hope: Visibilizing multilingual young people’s onto- epistemological brilliance in STEM education Shakhnoza Kayumova Despite reforms and interventions, racial and linguistic inequities persist within STEM education for youth from nondominant communities, reflecting entrenched deficit -based discourses and an ongoing framing of “lack” in students’ skills, identities, and engagement. This study contends that to disrupt these discourses, it is essential to interrogate the foundational logic of dominant epistemological and ontological (e.g., neutrality, objectivity, and universality) assumptions of disciplinary education. In t he U.S. public schools, emergent bilingual/multilingual youth tend to be entrenched with the deficit discourse that gets inscribed in tools of learning imbued with epistemic injustices (Harper & Kayumova, 2023) . The overwhelming emphasis is placed on academic language development, assuming that language is the primary mediator of learning, devoid of racial and colonial struggles (Takeuchi et al., 2022). As Escobar (2018) described, “[t]o think new thoughts, by implication, requires stepping out of the epist emic space of Western social theory and into the epistemic configurations associated with the multiple relation ontologies of worlds in struggle” (p. 68). The context and methodology The data for this study comes from a longitudinal research program aimed at understanding the intersection between language- based racialization and science identity development among multilingual youth from nondominant communities. Over six years, youth pa rticipated in a 2-week all-day summer program engaging in STEM projects tackling sustainability issues in their community. The overall goal of the program was to understand what it means to dismantle deficit-based discourses rooted in raciolinguistic ideologies by positioning multilingual youth in asset-based terms and subsequently equipping youth with tools to re-position themselves as cognitively advantaged. Within the program, we drew on theories of pluriverse and positioning to co-design asset- based learning spaces in which the students, teachers, and researchers were positioned as co -authors, co- researchers, and co-creators of knowledge. Given the longitudinal nature of the work, over the years we witnessed how local sustainability and environmental justice issues as a framework in the context of STEM allowed young people and teachers to explore the interconnectedness of inequities in disciplinary, social, and environmental spheres. Findings and contributions Our findings show that when youth’s home and community ways of being and knowing were centered as focal in disciplinary learning, their marginalized identities, as discussed by hooks (1991) and Wynter ( 1989), became integral to the radical possibilities  for reimagining social, cultural, and environmental futures based on care, relationality, and interdependence. In these spaces, as youth and educators engaged with issues like food justice, soil degradation, and environmental harms, their practice of relatio nality as an epistemic approach enabled them to “see, hear, and listen” to the living and more -than-living entities around them, grounding their disciplinary sensemaking in responsive, interconnected ways. Further examining the interconnected nature of these inequities, educators and youth began to explore how scientists and engineers are not merely documenting a world “out there” or designing detached solutions; rather, they are actively shaping reality through the models, definitions, and systems they cre ate. This awareness opened a space for reflecting on how the dehumanization experienced by multilingual youth within the culture of their schooling was connected to broader epistemic violence —where dominant assumptions of neutrality, objectivity, and unive rsality in disciplinary fields contribute to both the marginalization of human identities and the devaluation of nature (Kayumova & Dou, 2023). 2321 Youth, teachers and researchers came to see how the same epistemological and ontological frameworks that render certain forms of knowledge and experience invisible or invalid in their own lives also underpin a worldview that dehumanizes and exploits natural systems, framing both people and the environment as resources to be managed rather than as interconnected beings with intrinsic worth. Building on this critical lens, the youth developed innovative projects that embodied both disciplinary ingenuity and holistic worldviews. They created smart-sensing food conservation boxes and designed “living” smart homes that incorporated mushrooms as natural decomposers and filtration systems for water, all while exploring energy harvesting through fluid dynamics. We argue that deficit views of multilingual youth not only harm them by implying they must fragment their identities to engage in STEM but also alienate them “from the real socio -scientific challenges their communities face, failing to position them as essential contributors to urgently needed solutions” (Bang & Marin, 2015, p. 531). Through youth’s endeavors for environmental justices, they demonstrated how STEM practices could be reimagined to honor care, relationality and reciprocity reflecting a transformat ive ethic that not only challenged monoculture of dominant onto -epistemologies but also contributed to a collective hope for socially- environmentally just and thriving visions of living and futures. Land teaches us how we are related: Designing towards collective continuance through indigenous-led public schools Meixi, Racquel Banazsak, George Spears, Darci Ann Sigana, Lisa Ghost, Tyrell Anderson, Erma Mujic In a rapidly shifting and turbulent world, we have been deeply concerned with how schools might support a communities’ collective continuance—their collective capacities for a people to continue into the future (Whyte, 2017). We know that turbulent times are drastically uneven for Indigenous, Black, trans and queer, working class communities, and other people of color (Ladson -Billings, 2021) . Indigenous peoples have historically been through multiple pandemics —including the theft of land, boarding schools, and massive social and ecological destruction and upheaval —and therefore have millennia -old strategies for navigating the changes of the f uture (Meixi & Nzinga, 2024). Focusing on Storywalks as community members in the Franklin Avenue neighborhood, this paper explores Storywalks as a practice of reading, walking, and storying lands across families and educators (Marin & Bang, 2018). Given that a community's collective continuance is predicated on the moral relations and ethical commitments that members of a collective have to each other, we focus this paper on how storywalking revealed and renewed key relations embedded within Lands and the openings that these created for future-making together. The context and methodology The Storywalks featured here are hosted by families of Nawayee Center School, located in the historic Franklin Avenue neighborhood and American Indian Corridor in Minneapolis, Mní Sóta Makhóčhe (in Dakota, where the waters reflect the skies). We ask: How might centering families, lands and stories, reveal, enact, and renew community ethical expertise and relational commitments in Indigenous -led public school contexts? What might we learn from Indigenous educators and strategists who have been organizing and designing educational systems in spite of being occupied? Nawayee Center School is a contracted 7 th - 12th grade alternative school with a yearly enrollment of about 45 students. It is located in the heart of a diverse urban community in Minneapolis that is home to over 35,000 Native people), traditional and contemporary Dakota homelands that is also home to m any Ojibwe and Ho- Chunk people. The only remaining “survival school” (Davis, 2013) in the cities, Nawayee was born of vibrant intergenerational histories of almost 55 years of community- based activism and organizing since the late 1960s and was a particularly rich site for these walks together. Findings and contributions In our analysis, we draw from video data with students and community members as we moved around the lands and waterways in Minneapolis from September 2023—October 2024. Activities during this time included various guardian circle events, art workshops, and an Indigenous waterways camp. These events were designed and implemented by Nawayee Center School educators, community members and University researchers.  We particularly attended to critical historicity and future building through engagement with elders, exploring local Land and waterways, and learning Indigenous histories and practices (Bang & Vossoughi, 2016) . One key finding is that Land teaches us how we are related. On one level, this meant that land and waterscapes were holders of intergenerational life at multiple timescales - cosmological, geologic, plant time, and in imagining futures. Secondly, plants - and milkweed in specific, connected us in trans -Indigenous relations. Learning the specific roles and responsibilities of milkweed supported understanding connections across 2322 geographies specifically across educators in Chiapas, Mexico and Minneapolis. Finally, walking Lands was helpful in considering how we are restor(y)ing place - at ecological, political, and social levels - and the role of Indigenous presence in this endeavor. We find that Storywalking foundationally reorients learning at school towards community relationships to place and the renewal of the ethical bonds and expertise that reside in beloved community spaces. It is important for us to note however, it is the  ongoing history of relations prior to walking together, the kinds of relationalities enacted on these walks, and the possibilities of such renewal in the future were core to designing for our collective continuances as Franklin Avenue community members. As living relations are continually in flux and motion, more intentionally attending to our daily rounds disrupted traditional methods of learning by engaging whole -bodied intelligences with lands and each other (Betasamosake Simpson, 2013). Storywalks as a relational practice took seriously the rich tapestry of intergenerational family life at school and positioned the  school as a place of cultivating community hope, a kind of design towards collective continuance in loved places. Designing hopeful futures with a utopian methodology: The role of heterochrony Antti Rajala, Mike Cole, Moises Esteban Guitart The necessary shift from unsustainable, fossil -fuel-based practices to sustainable ones can approached as a learning process involving individuals, communities, and societies. This paper advances understanding of utopian |
| Developing Approaches to Racial Justice in the Learning Sciences | 2025 | ICLS 2025 | Bella Afra Boateng, Joe Curnow, Anny Chen, Abolitionist Futures Collective, Rachel Folger, Stephanie T. Jones, Sarah Priscilla Lee, Natalie Araujo Melo, Raesheena Kennedy, KIPP Columbus, Talia Leibovitz, Ashieda McKoy, Joseph L. Polman, Beatriz Salazar, Trang C. Tran | Bajaj, M. (2022) ‘Decolonial approaches to school curriculum for Black, Indigenous and other students of colour’. London Review of Education, 20 (1), 5. DOI: https://doi.org/10.14324/LRE.20.1.05. \| Blommaert, J., & Bulcaen, C. (2000). Critical Discourse Analysis. Annual Review of Anthropology, 29, 447–466. \| Delgado Bernal, D. 1998. Using a Chicana feminist epistemology in educational research. Harvard Educational Review 68(4), 555–582. De los Ríos, C. V., López, J., & Morrell, E. (2015). Toward a critical pedagogy of race: Ethnic studies and literacies of power in high school classrooms. Race and Social Problems, 7, 84-96. 2387 \| Gutierrez, K., Rymes, B., & Larson, J. (1995). Script, counterscript, and underlife in the classroom: James Brown versus Brown v. Board of Education. Harvard educational review, 65(3), 445-472. \| ross, kihana miraya, & Givens, J. R. (2023). The Clearing: On Black Education Studies and the Problem of “Antiblackness.”Harvard Educational Review, 93(2), 149–172. \| Womack, Y. L. (2013). Afrofuturism: The world of black sci-fi and fantasy culture. Chicago Review Press. 2389 | In this symposium, we build on the legacies and ongoing work of scholars across fields who have made room for research and teaching involving literacies and practices of racial justice. In recent years, we have seen even deeper engagement with these conversations within the learning sciences, as the shift toward addressing the political and ethical dimensions of learning has come into focus. Through this structured poster session, we expand theoretical underpinnings and attend to the ways that emerging scholars from different areas of the learning sciences are taking up the mantle. What does racial justice look like in the learning sciences? What does it look like, feel like, and sound like to advance racial justice in the learning sciences? What has racial justice meant? For whom? And, to what end? In this symposium, we build on the legacies and ongoing work of scholars across fields who have made room for research and teaching involving literacies and practices of racial justice. The session explores what it means to advance racial justice globally in this era, where the backlash against movements for racial repair and remediation has grown. Hostility against racial justice movements is evident in the systematic censorship of speech in education, increases in xenophobic policies, and escalating incidents of racialized violence interpersonally and by various state apparatuses. Moments like these require scholars of learning to mobilize our teaching, research, and service to advance justice projects (Politics of Learning Writing Collective, 2017), and to think carefully about the role of learning in addressing historicized power relations (Esmonde & Booker, 2017). However, racial justice has largely remained on the periphery of learning sciences work, and so in this moment of racialized backlash, we  look to scholarship and praxis from racial justice movements and literature to anchor our collective work in support of racial justice within the learning sciences. Our structured poster session invites participants to reflect on diverse racial justice traditions and interrogate if and how these theoretical keystones might generate new insights into learning toward justice. Since the learning sciences’ founding, some learning scientists have always been attentive to relations of power embedded in historicity and identity. From the earliest days, our predecessors have compellingly argued that racialization, ethnicity, class, and culture are key facets of learning ecologies that must be addressed analytically (Lee 1995; Rogoff, 1981; Gutierrez 1992, Nasir, 2002; Nasir & Hand, 2006; Rose 2001, Saxe, 1984). These foundations have created a fertile ground for further engagement across educational research fields that centrally address racialization, including critical race theory in education (e.g., Ladson -Billings & Tate, 1995), ethnic 2381 studies (e.g., Cammarota, 2016; de los Rios, et al., 2015), critical Indigenous pedagogies (e.g., Tachine & Nicolazzo, 2020), decolonial studies (e.g., Bajaj, 2022; Battiste, 2002; Zavala, 2016), and other specialized fields. In recent years, we have seen even deeper engagement with these conversations within the learning sciences, as the shift toward addressing the political and ethical dimensions of learning has come into focus (Esmonde & Booker, 2017). This growing scholarship emphasizes that learning is cultural  and embedded within current systems of injustice and that all conceptualizations of learning are connected to political systems (Madkins & McKinney de Royston, 2019). Within the learning sciences, attention to racial-ideological tensions in teaching and learning is generating important directions for learning scholarship. For instance, Philip (2011) and  Sengupta- Irving et al. (2021) reveal a need for racial literacy development, arguing that these skills are both distinctive from other disciplinary learning and needed in the context of deep, historical racial injustice. Another key focus of scholarship observes how learners imagine possibilities for racial justice through organizing and design (e.g., Pham & Philip, 2021; Arada et al., 2023). These approaches draw our collective attention to how learning and racial justice can and should be imbricated in our field to theorize learning in situ, where racial injustice is hegemonic and where calls to action from communities, movements, educators, and young people have urged us to take substantive action to remake racial relations of power. As we work to build racial justice scholarship in the learning sciences, we remain attentive to the relations, power, and processes that shape learning. We recognize that many scholars actively make space for discussions of race and racial justice within ICLS itself (Curnow, 2021; Marin et al., 2022; Jones et al., 2023; Rodgers, 2023; Shaw et al., 2023; Tzou et al., 2023; Leibovitz et al., 2024), contribute to ongoing dialogue about how we do this work, what counts as knowledge, and make way for alternative ways of being that are de-colonial, non-extractive, and meaningfully anti-racist. We affirm that such methodological considerations about how, why, for, and with whom we do research, are critical for advancing and understanding approaches to racial justice. As evidenced by the overlaps in our thinking, learning is inherently reflexive, community -centered, intersectional, and taking action toward an otherwise (Tachine & Nicolazzo, 2023). In this structured poster session, we take up critical perspectives, expand theoretical underpinnings to include new-to-the-field approaches to theorizing race, racialization, and racial justice, and attend to the ways that emerging scholars from different areas of the learning sciences are taking up the mantle. Collectively, we position our work within the learning sciences’ long -standing engagement with diversity and equity goals while actively integrating theoretical contributions from the broader fields of education, critical ethnic studies, Black studies, and other fields attuned to racial justice. We aim to offer a generative space to push ourselves and participants to make a deeper commitment to understanding racial relations of power across space, place, time, and condition, and centering these concerns in our design, theorization, and knowledge mobilization. Synergies within our work emphasize that racial justice is ultimately a relational endeavor. Through collective designs that center minoritized ways of knowing and being and pay attention to relations as both process and product (Bang & Vossoughi, 2017), we challenge hegemonic narratives and transform what learning could be toward justice. Facilitated process The symposium will be organized as a structured poster session, inviting participants to engage with various approaches to racial justice in the learning sciences and synthesize across a range of geographic, theoretical, and sociopolitical diversity. Our session will begin with initial framing by emerging researchers engaged with racial justice literature within and beyond the learning sciences. Each poster presenter will provide a brief overview of their work, then participants are invited to visit and engage more thoroughly with each of the eight posters at their own pace. Each poster will feature a raci al justice research design collaboration and respond to a series of questions rooted in racial justice theory and practice, including invitations to 1) Address the positioning of the community partnership and researcher(s), highlighting the role of relationality and identity in making specific knowledge production possible and productive, 2) Introduce the communities that the design collaborations are accountable to and describing the shared work in community, 3) Lay out the research questions and design goals, 4) Orient to particular traditions and lineages of racial justice theory and practice, and 5) Elaborate on how racial justice is prefigured through the communi ty design processes. Taken together, these posters demonstrate the diversity of racial justice work and bring a pedagogical stance to integrating racial justice scholarship into the learning sciences in theoretically grounded ways. We invite participants to engage the dialogue throughout the symposium. To this end, we will ask participants to consider: 1) As you engage with various explorations of racial justice research in this poster session, how are you conceptualizing racial justice in the learning sciences? 2) How might different conceptualizations of racial justice influence and impact your work and the communities you serve? 3) What questions do you have about racial justice research or how you might better facilitate racial justice in your own work? We will close with a synthesis of the overarching themes, offer insights on how we envision support within our field to foster 2382 meaningful and generative spaces for racial justice work and ignite discussion on what racial justice work might look like in participants’ research and design work. Working in good ways to prefigure abolition as racial justice Joe Curnow, Anny Chen Recently in Winnipeg, we have seen a proliferation of policing in everything from public libraries to grocery stores, justified through racialized language. We’ve seen similar “crime wave” discourses across North America, though in Winnipeg they have a distinctive anti -Indigenous framing (Wilt, 2024). However, a coalition of organizers have come together to fight for a city where investment in communities might create antiracist and anticolonial paths to community safety that do not rely on policing, prisons, and family separation. This coalition has struggled against mai nstream attitudes where safety means securitization and surveillance. How, then, do coalition organizers expand the imaginations of Winnipeggers beyond carceral solutions and design for abolitionist futures? The Abolitionist Futures Collective is a collective of prison and police abolitionist organizers in Winnipeg, MB, Canada. Our group includes people working on defunding police, prison rideshare, sex worker activists, library activists trying to remove secu rity screening from public libraries, Métis sovereignty groups, and others. We are a multiracial group with a plurality of non-binary/genderqueer folks. Joe (she/her) is a cis white woman with European and Latinx heritage. Anny (she/her) is a cis Canadian- born Chinese woman. Our research asks 1. How do we redefine safety toward community and away from colonial models of punishment, 2. How do we keep us safe, and 3. How do we facilitate politicization that enables participants in our workshops to imagine abolitionist futures? The Abolitionist Futures Collective works in Treaty 1 territory, the lands and waters of Nêhiyawak, Inninew, Anishinaabek, Oceti Sakowin Oyate, Michif, Anishininiiwak, and Denesuline people. This relationship calls on us to be in right relationship and attend to the ongoing colonial violence in Winnipeg. Racial justice is read through the long histories of Indigenous -led anticolonial resistance in Winnipeg (Sinclair, 2024), as well as Black feminist scholarship within prison and police abolition traditions (Dav is, 2011; Gilmore, 2007; Kaba, 2020). Racial justice shows up in our research design in two significant ways. First by drawing on lessons from Indigenous Elders, Knowledge Keepers, and community helpers, we have organized our work together through the Working in Good Ways framework (Ferland, Chen & Villagrán Becerra, n.d.). This approach offers resources for building strong relationships rooted in Indigenous ways of knowing and being while addressing historicized colonial power relations. Second, in our design we work to prefigure (Maeckelbergh, 2005; Curnow & Uttamchandani, 2022) abolitionist politics (Gilmore, 2007), where building relationships and community is a central outcome and process, where we plan for accessibility and attempt to integrate Dis ability Justice into our approach, where we strive to be in anti/decolonial relationship, and where we work to center racial justice. Getting real with racial justice: Lessons learned from technology spaces Stephanie T. Jones In this study, I focused on how Black computing practitioners understood the connections between Black Life and computing. I began with the theoretical perspective of Afrofuturism for thinking about potential visions for the future related to Black Life and Computing. However recurring refrains in the interviews were 1) the notion of what we can and should expect in a capitalist society, 2) the ways particular forms of Blackness are appreciated or denied, and 3) the ways historical events work as a guide for current decision making in the computing space. To describe the particular histories participants brought up, I offered the theoretical perspective of Plantation Prophecy, to discuss the ways the plantation is reified in society as a past, current, and “hopefully” future model for living globally. The early stages of this work were born out of a desire to more closely conduct analyses of how race and racialization shape the experience and design of learning environments. As an early career learning scientist, I struggled to find literature that talk ed about race and racism as shaping technology education. As a student of engineering and education in the US, I have been repeatedly impacted by the way the education system selects particular models of being as acceptable for those who are negatively racialized. During this time I also didn’t experience education on how race operates in engineering and education spaces - and thus had limited awareness of engineering as a political profession and as one where the work of race was to be a “good” minority (J ones & melo, 2021). In particular, this meant being under -aware of how my positionality as a Black femme was being shaped as a commodity for profit. 2383 In order to get real about racial justice, we have to get real about how race operates structurally and to consider where we would like to focus our energies if not racism (Bell, 1992). To do this, I integrate lessons from Black study (Morrison, 1987; Bell, 1992; Hartman, 2002; ross & Givens, 2023). I explore how Plantation Prophecy can be a useful analytical perspective for being racially literate when it comes to technology development. I think of racial justice not as a singular event but as an ongoing re -evaluation of commitments, relationships, and ways of knowing that take us away from subject/object relations. In this poster, I offer insights through Critical Discourse Analysis (Blommaert & Bulcaen, 2000; Ohito & Nyachae, 2019) of interviews to reveal ways Black computing practitioners were talking about racial justice and their potential role as a part of their work. Further,  I explore how practitioners became and are working to become racially literate given that these discussions rarely happen in computing. For example, overwhelmingly practitioners said “no” when asked if race had ever been discussed in computing classroom e nvironments - and how this lack of instruction led to moments of uncertainty in how to approach times of great contention, daily activities, and tokenization. Cross-cultural youth organizing and intergenerational learning Sarah Priscilla Lee How does intergenerational learning offer a generative framework for understanding how learning and teaching in a cross -cultural youth organizing group shows up and unfolds? How are relations across people and places situated within, shaped by, and engaged across sociopolitical, cultural, and historical realities on a municipal, national, and global scale (Tivaringe & Kirshner, 2021)? I look across two out-of-school settings: A youth summer program (ages 5–19) within a nonprofit refugee resettlement organization and Eternal Youth, a youth organizing group in a large midwestern city composed of Black, Latinx, South Asian, and Afghan refugee youth from middle school to college. In both spaces, participants represent a plethora of identities often reduced by labels like “youth” and "refugee." In Eternal Youth, I analyze how participants engage in meaning-making practices that resist hegemonic narratives and create liberatory possibilities on their terms. By examining how participants build relationships and structures by drawing on existing skills and experiences, I highlight how youth navigate transnational complexities and (re)shape the stories and relationships to each other and  to the spaces as woven across both spaces. Finally, in the design and facilitation of the summer program, we worked toward co- design to center students’ interests and support facilitator training rooted in care and culturally responsive practices (Ladson- Billings, 1995). This builds on the critical discourse around refugee education, focusing on the often-overlooked political subjectivities of refugee youth and families (Sukarieh & Tannock, 2014). At its core, this work examines the contradictions and possibilities of transnational identity and intergenerational learning toward building collective power and engaging education as a practice of freedom (hooks, 1994). Becoming racially literate as racial justice praxis Talia Leibovitz I conducted a four year ethnographic study analyzing a multi -racial group of middle-class parents organizing for racial justice in Oakland, California as a cis hetero white woman with eastern European  heritage who engages in anti-racist racial justice organizing in the K -12 education system. I utilize an interdisciplinary framework of critical policy analysis and socio-cultural theories of learning to consider how people learn and strategize toward racial justice amidst the local political economy they are organizing within. My research places education policy in conversation with the learning sciences, pushing the boundaries of both disciplines to demonstrate that education policy scholars and scholars of learning must understand how and what policy actors learned about education policy and racial justice. Much of the existing research on middle -class parents across racial groups organizing for racial justice in education illustrates how despite the best of intentions (Lewis & Diamond, 2015), these parents often reproduce the very inequalities they aim to ameliorate. Building from this research, I consider how middle -class parents across racial groups engage in moment-to-moment interactions that shape their learning for racial justice in ways that afford a rearticulation of racialized ideologies. I draw from Stuart Hall’s understanding that ideologies are constantly contested and at times, transformed (Hall, 1996). Societal transformat ion occurs via rearticulation where ideological connections are disrupted, and new ones are created (Hall, 1996). Hall’s framing of articulation is particularly relevant given this study’s focus on how a group of parents came to contest commonly held racialized ideologies of school quality in ways that not only shaped their learning of racialization but also their organizing strategies for racial justice. I also orient to racial justice through Scott and Bajaj’s (2022) use of racialization to analytically consider how people or groups come to be classified beyond identity, and how these 2384 categories are connected to political, social, and economic dynamics that have consequences for people’s life opportunities. I developed an original conceptual framework that integrated theories of political economy and socio- cultural approaches to learning in order to capture the macro conditions that shape inequality in an urban district, while also documenting and learning from the moment-to-moment interactions through which people make sense of inequality and organize toward racial justice. This interdisciplinary framework allowed me to ask how the socio-political context of a school district informs the discourse and policies of educational efforts and how people learn about racial equity through their organizing. The design of my study as an ethnographic case study allowed me to analyze learning over time as organizers for racial justice responded to political events that unfolded in the school district. The multiple years of observations were a critical design of my study given that racial literacy is not a location one arrives at but rather a process that occurs over time in a non-linear way, This contributed to a rich analysis of how people collectively became racially literate through their  organizing and how their collective racial literacy informed new organizing strategies to respond to the political economy of the school district. “We be knowing”: Uplifting black women’s knowledge toward justice Nicollette D. Mitchell My research, through the Black Women in Geoscience Education project, centers Black women in the United States who have earned post -secondary degree(s) in geosciences. I currently seek our (Black -woman- geoscientists) own ontological, epistemological, and methodological ways of conceiving disciplinary learning as a form of racial justice. Such positioning is made necessary because the geosciences, which has been imperative in describing Earth’s systems and processes, has remained the least demographically diverse of all science, engineering, mathematics, and technology fields (Bernard and Cooperdock, 2018). To elevate the experiences of Black women in this context, I have adopted an intersectional frame (Crenshaw 1991) to consider  structural, political, and representational aspects of Black women’s geoscience experiences. This structural, political, and representational approach to research helps to further elucidate forms of thriving in a racialized -gendered space that has historically positioned Black women in a deficit- oriented way. My goal through this work is to engage in  Black feminist praxis – and interweaving of theory, practice, and methods -  which empowers Black women through the disruption of oppressive ideas and practices (Porter and colleagues, 2023). I am a Black woman immigrant mother, scholar, and former paleoclimatologist who is currently enrolled in a doctoral program within the learning sciences. I approach racial justice from a Black feminist ontological space (Collins, 2022), which urges me to believe in the liberatory capacity of learning. Said otherwise, teaching and learning have always been important to me, my mothers, aunties, and sisters and will always be. I cannot divorce my understanding of how and what is learned from that inheritance. I find myself here in community with other scholars who conceive of learning and racial justice as interrelated formations. This exercise, of th inking through and articulating what racial justice means in learning sciences, gives me hope. In conceiving of what racial justice means in the context of my scholarly contributions to learning science, it is imperative that the work acknowledges and explores (for me, participants, and the reader) both/and (Collins, 2022) – the nuances of being simultaneously marginalized and agentic.   Within the context of the Black Women  in Geoscience Education Project, that means that the research design, analysis, and discussion centers are responsive to the needs of Black women. Creating space for Black women to speak to their/our own experiences as knowledgeable is a part of disrupting epistemic injustice about what counts as disciplinary learning. In pursuit of justice, then, I work to amplify that which is already known in ways that refute colonial logics that requi re extraction from multiply marginalized people for the use of the academy. Prototyping black speculative justice learning among future educators at a PWI Ashieda McKoy With an increasing number of students of color in schools and a largely white teaching population, we in the learning sciences (have and still) need to be thinking about how we study and design more inclusive and justice - centered learning environments for our young people and their future educators (NASEM, 2024). Here, I believe that Afrofuturism as an aesthetic, pedagogy, and sensibility for design, offers a critically innovative lens to study and craft justice-centered learning for all educators. I point to a Black speculative, namely Afrofuturist, orientation of racial justice that situates justice work in exploring how race and diverse cultural experiences affect pedagogical 2385 reality, knowledge, design, and (learning) futures (Cann, 2016; Womack, 2013) and, more importantly how, in community with each other, we can and should (re)imagine and (re)invent the alternative technologies, realities and futures of our Black freedom dreams (Carey, 2020; Faison and McArthur, 2020; Love, 2019). Noting this largely white femme educator population, my project especially involves making sure that white educators realize they are important and necessary factors in concretizing the just (learning) futures we need. This study takes place in an emerging educator course at a primarily white institution (PWI) whose educators go on to serve largely urban Black and Latinx students. I ask: How can future educator environments be designed to support the development of pedagogical theories and practices oriented toward just Black futures? For this dissertation project, I redesigned a teacher learning ecology using Afrofuturist principles of learning and design and sociocultural theories of learning. Primarily organized around four main Afrofuturist learning guides–justice-centered, future-oriented, technology-mediated, and collective, my course design supports future educators in grappling and experimenting with new pedagogical theories and practices for more just learning futures that are developed in iteration with youth, are justice -centered and tech-mediated. As such, this course and dissertation study is specifically designed to center the theories and practices of Black, Brown, and Queer thinkers, educators, and youth, to understand justice -centered learning through cycles of iterative design where future educators and youth “prototype” new ideas and theories for more liberatory futures. Ultimately, the (re)design of this course offe rs new windows into the design of a justice -centered teacher learning environment that is offered within the context of PWI university-based teacher education. Latina testimonios of failure: Trenzas of failure, learning, and survivance in the Latina experience Beatriz Salazar Over the last 20 years in the field of learning sciences, there has been an increasing amount of research surrounding failure and the experiences of failure as opportunities for learning. However, what has often been left out of the conversation, until more recently, has been the ways identity impacts the ways learners experience failure. In the United States, girls and young women of color often receive negative consequences as a result of asking questions in the classroom (Annamma et al, 2019). Instead, perfection and a lack of questions are rewarded in the education system, despite the research supporting opportunities for failure, mistakes , and questions (Kapur, 2008; McNamara, 2001; Van Lehn et al., 2003). Guided by the framework of trenzas and greñas (Montoya, 1994) and implementing testimonio (Delgado-Bernal, 1998) as a method, this dissertation explores how Latinas in the United States experience self - identified failures and the learning that stems from these experiences. Much like Montoya’s experience in trenzas and greñas, Latinas and people of color, wear masks that hide our greñas, our imperfections in our braided lives. This is a notion that is especially true for those who are children of immigrants,  who often believe that failure is not an option after their parents gave up so much for them (Romano, 2022). Together, the researcher and the collaborators learn how to explore this racial justice issue through a collaborative, decolonized methodology. Without these masks, our true and whole selves might not be accepted by others. This work seeks to challenge the notion that to be imperfect means you are a failure. Without mistakes and the room to self -reflect, process, and re -mediate our errors in true vulnerability, people of color are robbed of the opportunity to learn, while the system is robbed of the opportunity to acknowledge its wrongs. As a Latina daughter of immigrant parents growing up in the US, I challenge the notion that “failure is not an option” or that to fail means to become a failure that youth of color and children of immigrants carry. We are not perfect, nor are we meant to be. We too deserve the opportunity to learn and grow. Facilitating learning with data for racial justice Trang C. Tran, Joseph L. Polman, Katherine Miller, Raesheena Kennedy, Rachel Folger We are a group of interdisciplinary education researchers and classroom educators, from a project named DataPBL. In our codesign effort, two teachers (Kennedy & Folger) engaged with data specialists, curriculum developers, and learning scientists to build data-focused addendums, including curating and customizing datasets, for existing curricula that blend Social Studies and ELA content. Our work serves students who are attending Marbles Expeditionary, a charter K -8 school located in the midwest U.S. serving predominantly Black low - and middle-income students, and Oak Park Expeditionary, a public K -12 school located in the northeastern U.S., serving predominantly Latine American students, and students from immigrant families. Across their middle school classrooms, we observed how  instructors introduced and scaffolded data practices that were grounded in 2386 and entwined with their commitment to promote racial equity and racial justice. We co -developed an interest in exploring the sociopolitical implications of teaching, learning, and creating with data. Specifically, how may classroom educators facilitate learning with data for racial justice? Our approach to racial justice issue focused on 1) constructing high -quality curricula that center and serve our own students to unsettle a normalized reality in which Black and Brown youth are seldomly prioritized in learning materials (Dumas, 2014; McKinney de Royston et al., 2021) and 2) interrogating and counteracting the systemic neglect of Japanese American injustice and Asian Americans across K -20 curricula (An, 2016). We describe two teachers' adaptations and enactments of a curriculum module that examines the mass incarceration of Japanese Americans during World War II. In Folger’s class, students studied the history of immigration to the US in the 20th century, the role immigration and citizenship played in the experience of Japanese incarceration, and current trends in migration and detention, engaging multiple datasets to explore the question “Who gets to be an American?” Kennedy’s data lessons delved into “sense of belonging” as an enduring dilemma affecting generations of immigrants and racially marginalized communities. Students worked with datasets to unveil the stories of Japanese American prisoners before, during, an d after incarceration and the current -day experience of refugees in students’ local state; furthermore, students collected their own data embracing the sense of belonging at their own school community. In this poster, we examine the range of activities and interactions with data that our classroom educators and their students employed to challenge systemic violence and deficit narratives on the Japanese and Asian American communities. We explore the role that youth's intersectional identities played in their emotional responses and perspective -taking regarding various racial injustices, including those affecting immigrant, refugee communities, and their own racialized school communities.
